2	
3	## Context
4	
5	The LoginID take-home is being run as a demonstration of AI-architecture capability: a root PM agent (Dana Whitfield / team-lead) plus five persistent track leads (Naomi Voss 01, Marcus Ilori 02, Renata Cole 03, Theo Bergman 04, Priya Nandakumar 05), each with a profile-gen persona, each working from its own track directory. All five tracks have finished their first-wave design docs; all code has been deliberately wiped; the project is paused in a design/planning phase. Marcus has delivered the team's agreed Claude-architecture approach in `02-ai-security-architecture/planning-approach.md` (model/effort per role, orchestrator-worker with bounded two-party exceptions, the "receiver can act without re-deriving" hand-off standard, dependency order 01/02/05 → 03 → 04, and a thin root `PLANNING.md` board).
6	
7	Warren now wants the formal planning phase run across the whole org, with three additions to Marcus's approach:
8	
9	1. Every track lead must "hire" (create) a sub-team of agents and state each hire's function. Hires must vary in **personality and archetype**, not just viewpoint — variance across the org, not just within a team (a serious principles-based architect paired with a silly innovative one, etc.).
10	2. **Every** agent created must run profile-gen so it has a personality, professional history, and technical background. Profile pictures are optional and may be produced by background jobs.
11	3. The orchestration patterns must make heavy use of those personas to drive debate and diversity of thought.
12	
13	Leads may spawn their sub-teams into new tmux panes. Everyone goes into plan mode and plans first; Jira stories in project `LT` (uplifttech.atlassian.net, cloudId `db488eae-d6cb-4100-92a9-e7a4519fc176`, issue types Epic/Story/Task/Bug/Subtask) come **after** planning. No implementation code until Warren gives a separate go-ahead.
14	
15	Everything in `/Users/warrenthompson/Source/LoginID` is part of the submission, so the org scaffolding (casting registry, personas, agent definitions) must read as intentional, presentable AI-architecture work — not scratch.

17	## Inputs this plan builds on (already decided — do not re-litigate)

23	## Verified mechanics that shape the design
24	
25	profile-gen (`~/.claude/skills/profile-gen` → symlink to `~/Source/ProfileGenSkill`):
26	- `--output file --assets tracked --root <track-dir>` writes `<track-dir>/profiles/<slug>/<slug>.md` with **no** CLAUDE.md line; unlimited personas per directory (`scripts/profilegen/storage.py:98-110`, `write_profile.py:121-142`).
27	- `show_profile.py --autostart-only` displays **every** persona that has a `profile-gen:start` marker in the root `CLAUDE.md`, in file order (`show_profile.py:219-224`). Therefore hires must **never** be added as CLAUDE.md markers — only the five leads and Dana keep markers. Hires are displayed on demand with `show_profile.py --profile <path> --root <track-dir>`.
28	- Schema has no history/backstory field (`assets/profile.schema.json`, `additionalProperties: false`); `personality` is unlimited free prose, so history + technical background live there.
29	- `write_profile.py` only checks the `image` **key** exists, never the file (`write_profile.py:63,90-92`) → personas can be created **text-first** with a pre-declared image path, and the PNG generated later into that path by a background queue. Display degrades gracefully to name-only until the file exists.
30	- `generate_image.py --workflow <any path> --seed N` — the existing validated workflow `profiles/dana-whitfield/comfyui-workflow.json` is reusable for every portrait; it is never mutated.
31	- Global `SessionStart` hook (`~/.claude/settings.json`) runs `show_profile.py --root "${CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR:-$PWD}" --autostart-only` before a subagent's first turn, so every new pane shows Dana until the agent runs `show_profile.py --profile <own>`; every spawn prompt must include that command after its `cd`.
32	
33	Harness (confirmed against the Claude Code docs — sub-agents.md, agent-teams.md, settings-reference.md, hooks.md):
34	- Agent definitions `.claude/agents/<slug>.md` support frontmatter `name`, `description`, `model` (`sonnet|opus|haiku|fable|inherit`), `tools`/`disallowedTools`, `permissionMode`, `skills`, `memory`, `maxTurns`, and a `hooks` block scoped to that agent's lifetime. **No effort key exists**, and teammates inherit the lead's effort level (Warren's `high`). The only per-model lever is `modelSettings.<model>.effortLevel` in `.claude/settings.json`. Consequence: Marcus's effort tiers are implemented as *inherit high for sonnet/opus/fable* plus `haiku → low` for throwaway research fan-out; the sonnet-medium vs sonnet-high distinction collapses to high (small cost, errs upward on the tracks that matter). Model per hire is still pinned in its definition.
35	- The per-agent `hooks` block is the permanent fix for the "every pane shows Dana" problem — with one correction found during Phase 0: agent frontmatter supports only `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, and `Stop` (no `SessionStart`), and `$CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR` is not available in subagent hooks. So each hire's definition carries a `PreToolUse` hook with `matcher: ".*"` and `once: true` that runs `show_profile.py --clear` then `show_profile.py --profile <own persona> --root <track-dir>` (absolute paths) on the hire's first tool call, then removes itself. The spawn prompt keeps the same command as a fallback.
36	- Nested spawning works but is capped at 3 levels by default (`CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_SUBAGENT_SPAWN_DEPTH`). Hierarchy therefore stays **team-lead → lead → hire**; hires do not spawn subagents — any research fan-out is done by the lead. The harness opens a tmux pane per spawned agent.
37	- Teammate plan mode **auto-approves**: a teammate's `ExitPlanMode` is approved in the lead's session without review (agent-teams.md). So plan mode is used for what it is good at — forcing a written plan before any edits — and the real gate is manual: the lead copies its approved plan into `<track-dir>/PLAN.md`, messages team-lead, and does **not** hire until team-lead replies with an explicit "hiring go" after reviewing the roster against the variance rules.

48	   - **`git init`** at the root with a milestone commit at the end of every phase (Phase 0 scaffolding, each approved plan, each completed hiring wave, each debate artifact set, the consistency pass, Jira export). The commit history is the most credible "how the AI workflow actually ran" artifact the submission can carry; commit messages follow the repo attribution convention (Co-Authored-By Claude Fable 5.1 + session link), which itself documents the AI involvement LoginID asked about. Commits only at milestones, never mid-task, and never `.claude/settings.local.json`, image caches, or secrets. A `.gitignore` covers `.claude/settings.local.json` and `*.lock`.

50	1. **`PLANNING.md`** (root, new) — exactly the thin board Marcus specified: one row per track — `Track | Lead | Status | Blocking on | Blocked by | Planning notes (relative path) | Hires (count/registered) | Last updated` — plus a **Service boundaries** section that 03 owns and 04 reads (initially "TBD — set by 03 during planning"). 52	2. **`CASTING.md`** (root, new) — the org-wide casting registry, hiring rules, and orchestration-pattern catalog. Team-lead owns the rules and palette; each lead owns its own registry rows. Sections:
53	
54	   - *Archetype palette* (13 — enough slack for a no-repeat-within-team rule at ~11 hires):
55	
56	     | Archetype | Temperament sketch | Blind spot | Pairs well with |
57	     |---|---|---|---|
58	     | Principled Architect | standards-first, cites RFC section numbers, reasons threat-model-down | over-specifies; mistakes thoroughness for done | security controls, contracts |
59	     | Tinkerer (silly & innovative) | playful, analogy-driven, sketches three odd alternatives before defending one | novelty bias; underweights boring proven controls | any decision that needs alternatives |
60	     | Designated Skeptic | argues against the room by default, politely | objections without a counter-proposal | authz scoping, abstraction choices |
61	     | Consensus Weaver | finds the shared premise, writes the synthesis | papers over real disagreement | decision records, hand-off prose |
62	     | Spreadsheet | wants a number or a table; distrusts adjectives | measures only what is measurable | sizing, TTLs, retention windows |
63	     | Storyteller | thinks in user journeys and narrative arcs | charming beats correct | product framing, README, use cases |
64	     | Veteran | "I watched this fail in prod in 2019" | fights the last war; pattern-matches too fast | token lifecycle, migrations, ops |
65	     | Newcomer | bright, asks why, assumes no jargon | doesn't know what's already settled | legibility tests on every hand-off |
66	     | Detail Hawk | nullable-vs-required, off-by-one, error semantics | cannot see the forest | schema, DDL, code review |
67	     | Systems Cartographer | draws boundaries and flows; sees interactions | abstracts past the concrete | multi-DB abstraction, service topology |
68	     | Adversary | thinks like an attacker, enjoys it | sees threats everywhere, no prioritization | connector, search API |
69	     | Minimalist | "what if we didn't build this?" | deletes something load-bearing | infra, scope control |
70	     | Enthusiast | bold, optimistic, wants the promising thing tried | discounts operational cost | counterweight to deadpan/cautious leads |
71	
72	   - *Variance rules* (team-lead enforces at the hiring gate, reading all rosters together): (1) no archetype more than **2× org-wide**, never twice within a team; (2) no two leads hire the same archetype for the same function (two "Detail Hawk reviewers" is duplication, not variance); (3) every team includes its lead's **temperament opposite** — Naomi (curious narrative analyst) → Spreadsheet; Marcus (rigorous citer) → Tinkerer; Renata (clarity-over-cleverness shipper) → Enthusiast; Theo (deadpan minimalist) → Enthusiast or Newcomer; Priya (schema-first PII guardian) → Storyteller; (4) org-wide at least **three** hires tagged playful+bold, because zero leads are; (5) each roster spans at least two temperament axes (serious↔playful, cautious↔bold).
73	
74	   - *Sizing* (Warren's call: the larger org) — 3–4 hires per track, 18 total, **hard cap 18**. With 13 archetypes at ≤2× org-wide there are 26 slots, so the cap fits without repeats inside any team. Suggested cast, leads may adjust within the rules: 01 (3) → Storyteller, Spreadsheet (opposite), Designated Skeptic; 02 (4) → Adversary, Principled Architect, Tinkerer (opposite), Designated Skeptic; 03 (4) → Detail Hawk (code reviewer), Veteran, Enthusiast (opposite), Systems Cartographer (Go layout / service topology); 04 (3) → Newcomer, Enthusiast (opposite), Minimalist (scope guard); 05 (4) → Systems Cartographer (multi-DB abstraction), Storyteller (opposite), Detail Hawk (DDL nullability — a different function from 03's, so allowed), Spreadsheet (retention windows). Archetypes used twice must have different functions. Leads may still wear a "hat" themselves in three-hats runs rather than hiring for it.
75	
76	   - *Persona-writing standard* — 90–150 words in `personality`, in this order: temperament (two adjectives and how they show up in a meeting); decision style; **what they specifically push back on**; plausible history (two prior roles, generic employers, no real names); technical background tied to the function; **their own blind spot stated plainly** so the lead can pair against it; how they behave in disagreement. One verbal habit allowed (a recurring question, an analogy style); no repeated catchphrases. Avoid: real people/companies, checkable credentials, stereotypes, sarcasm at a person's expense, profanity, emoji, appearance (that belongs in the image prompt). Two worked examples are included verbatim in `CASTING.md` — the Tinkerer ("a token cache is a coat check that burns the coats every fifteen minutes… pushes back on 'that's the standard approach' whenever nobody can say what the standard protects against; blind spot: novelty bias") and the Principled Architect ("will not accept a control without naming the attack it stops… blind spot: over-specification; changes position when shown evidence, and says so") — so the contrast Warren asked for is concrete.
77	
78	   - *Orchestration pattern catalog* (full protocols; cost in agent-turns = one hire invocation + reply):
79	     - **Adversarial pair** — Proposer drafts; Designated Skeptic writes numbered objections; Proposer revises; lead rules. 3 turns + lead. Artifact: decision with "objections considered". *Use:* search-API authz scoping (`profile:search` vs `profile:read:own`, object-level policy); the `Search()` query shape in `multi-db-strategy.md`.
80	     - **Red team / blue team** — Adversary writes an attack tree against one surface; Principled Architect answers each leaf with a control or an accepted risk; lead scores residual risk. 2–3 turns. Artifact: attack-tree table appended to `connector-security.md`. *Use:* the connector token lifecycle only (TTL, at-rest encryption, per-vendor keying, fail-closed). Waste on DAO CRUD or a Dockerfile.
81	     - **Three hats** — Tinkerer (optimist), Veteran (pessimist), Spreadsheet/Minimalist (pragmatist) each write ≤300 words in parallel; lead synthesizes. 3 turns + lead. *Use:* multi-DB abstraction (per-driver implementations vs dialect branches vs codegen); Go layout (one binary vs two).
82	     - **Rotating devil's advocate** — a standing "Objection" seat that moves per decision so nobody becomes "the negative one". 1 turn per decision. *Use:* Renata's implementation calls (error semantics, context propagation, test doubles). Cheapest pattern; run often.
83	     - **Structured written debate** — position → rebuttal → lead synthesis, both positions preserved. 3 turns. *Use:* Naomi's central framing (password baseline: deliberate simplification or contradiction at a passkey company?); Marcus's core claim in `ai-workflow-narrative.md`.
84	     - **Newcomer's question** — Newcomer reads a finished hand-off cold and lists what it could not act on without re-deriving; author fixes. 1–2 turns. *Use:* every 05→03 and 02→03 hand-off, the README, `PLANNING.md`. Directly tests the hand-off standard; highest-ROI pattern in the project.
85	     - *Never debate what the assignment text fixes* (endpoint paths, JSON bodies). Theo's track: one hire, one Newcomer's-question pass on 03's config surface and 02's secrets inventory, nothing else.
86	
87	   - *Registry table* — one row per hire, appended by the hiring lead after team-lead's "hiring go": `Slug | Name | Track | Function | Archetype | Temperament tags | Model | Patterns & role | Persona path | Agent def path | Portrait (queued/done)`.

codegen is orthogonal, 03's choice; **requirement on 03: one conformance suite run against every backend** — SQLite `LIKE` is case-insensitive vs Postgres case-sensitive, so the documented parity gap is a correctness gap, not a performance one; S2 ruled `05-data-ops/decisions/search-query-shape.md` — 8/8 objections accepted; adopted principle: **every caller-visible guarantee names an enforcement site (DDL constraint, index, normalization, or conformance test) — one with no site is a hope, not a guarantee**; **S1 Go-shaped contract stable in `05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md`** — 03 may start the DAO; S6 cold read (Saoirse, deliberately kept unfamiliar) returned 14 blocking items, all accepted and fixed: `Update`/`Upsert` are FULL REPLACES (nil NULLs the column), `Upsert` conflicts on id only (identity resolution is the connector's problem), error translation revised to structured codes + our own named constraints (SQLite has no SQLSTATE), `ErrInvalidArgument` added for CHECK violations, `is_active` enforcement site named; S5 closed in `05-data-ops/pii-governance.md` — retention windows (24mo direct / 30d cache / 7d orphan, all proposed pending product-legal), deletion mechanism, and the health check that matters: **alert on age-of-oldest-surviving-row per class, since a stopped sweep produces no error at all**; `deletion_log` added to the schema, PII-free by construction; S4 final and delivered to 04 (`migration-approach.md`) — objection turn overturned R2's *reasoning* while the requirement stood: a txn-scoped advisory lock releases between goose files, so it cannot cover a multi-file run; factory mismatch closed with 03 direct (two-party): `dao.Repository` composite exposing `Profiles()`/`Credentials()`/`Methods()`, `dao.New` signature unchanged — cited in S1 | 4 / 4 / 4 | 2026-09-12 |

30	## Service boundaries (owned by 03, read by 04)
31	
32	Set by 03 during Phase 1 planning (full reasoning: `03-engineering-delivery/PLAN.md` §4).
33	
34	- **Two binaries**, not one: `cmd/api-service` (Q1 DAO + Q2 REST API, client-facing, bearer-token auth) and `cmd/idp-connector` (Q3, outbound-only to vendors) — different threat models/auth surfaces per 02, independent scaling needs, mirrors the assignment's own Q2/Q3 split.
35	- **Package layout:**
36	  ```
37	  cmd/api-service/main.go
38	  cmd/idp-connector/main.go
39	  internal/dao/        # Repository composite interface (Profiles/Credentials/Methods) + postgres/cockroachdb/sqlite implementations
40	  internal/model/      # shared structs: UserProfile, UserCredential
41	  internal/api/         # REST handlers, router, auth middleware
42	  internal/connector/   # /auth, /identity client + handlers
43	  internal/config/      # env-var config loading, shared by both binaries
44	  internal/app/         # shared bootstrap (route registration, config wiring) both cmd/*/main.go wrap thinly
45	  ```
46	- **Config surface:** environment variables only (12-factor: config lives in the environment, not in checked-in files) — `DB_DRIVER` (postgres|cockroachdb|sqlite), `DB_DSN`, `DB_DSN_FILE` (file-mount path; takes precedence over `DB_DSN` when both are set, per 02's `handoff-04-secrets.md` S7 revision — a DSN's embedded password is as exposed by `kubectl describe`/crash dumps/env-in-child-process as any other secret in that inventory, so `DB_DSN` is now a transitional fallback, not a permanent equal option), `HTTP_ADDR`, `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER` = `https://auth.loginid-takehome.internal` / `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` = `loginid-api-service` (literal values per `handoff-03-auth.md` — not secrets, safe to ship in config), `IDP_ABC_BASE_URL`, `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_ID`/`IDP_ABC_CLIENT_SECRET`. No flags, no config files.
47	- **DAO driver-selection mechanism:** a factory `dao.New(driver, dsn string) (dao.Repository, error)` chosen at startup from `DB_DRIVER`. `dao.Repository` is a composite interface — `Profiles() ProfileRepository`, `Credentials() CredentialRepository`, `Methods() AuthMethodRepository` — matching 05's three-way split in `multi-db-strategy.md`, kept deliberately distinct (not one flat interface) because it's a load-bearing PII/credential-separation control per `05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`: a handler has to reach for the credential accessor on purpose. Each backend package returns one struct satisfying the composite. Factory signature and `DB_DRIVER` selection are unchanged; the three sub-interfaces' method signatures finalize once 05's contract lands.
48	- **Stable for 04 to build against:** the two-binary split, the `cmd/` entrypoint names, the env-var config approach and variable names above, and the `dao.New(driver, dsn)` factory signature. **Not yet stable:** internal DAO implementation details, ORM/query-builder choice, handler-package internals.
49	- **Note for 04, from the Go-layout three-hats ruling (`03-engineering-delivery/decisions/go-layout-debate.md`):** "two binaries" is a *process* boundary, not a claim about host/deploy placement — whether `api-service` and `idp-connector` land on the same node is 04's call, not implied by this section. If they're colocated, `idp-connector`'s outbound-call failure isolation from `api-service` depends on 04's resource limits, not just the process split. Separately, S4 now requires `context.WithTimeout` on every outbound vendor call and a bounded connection pool/backoff on token-refresh retries — not a 04 concern, but flagged here so 04 doesn't assume connector-side backpressure is handled elsewhere.
50	
51	## Dependency order (from `planning-approach.md` §2)
52	
53	1. First wave, in parallel: 01, 02, 05 — 05 is on the critical path (it hard-blocks 03's DAO and partially blocks 04).
54	2. 03 split start: scaffolding may begin immediately; DAO once 05's Go-shaped contract is stable; middleware once 02's receiver-shaped auth hand-off lands.
55	3. 04 last, gated on the three inputs named in its row.

1	# Planning Approach: Claude-Architecture Recommendations for This Team
2	
3	Author: Marcus Ilori (02-ai-security-architecture), synthesizing input gathered directly from all four other track owners — naomi-voss (01), renata-cole (03), theo-bergman (04), priya-nandakumar (05) — plus this track's own perspective and `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md`. Where the tracks disagreed or emphasized different things, I say so rather than averaging them into a mush; where they independently converged (which happened more than I expected), I note that too, because five agents with separate contexts landing on the same answer is itself weak evidence the answer is right.
4	
5	Throughout, I distinguish Anthropic-documented guidance from my own judgment applied to this specific project. Where I say "Anthropic recommends," that traces to a specific source in `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md`. Where I say "my view" or "I'd recommend," that's this project's reasoning, not a citation.
6	
7	## 1. Models and effort levels, by role
8	
9	The knobs: model ∈ {fable, opus, sonnet, haiku}, effort ∈ {low, medium, high, xhigh, max}. Warren has already set the orchestrator (team-lead) to fable at high. My reasoning below takes that as fixed and reasons about everyone else relative to it.
10	
11	**Orchestrator / PM (team-lead) — fable, high.** Confirmed correct, not just accepted. The orchestrator holds the only cross-track state that matters (scope boundaries, status, "who's blocked on whom") and makes judgment calls that propagate into every track if wrong — a boundary misjudged here (e.g., letting infra silently absorb a decision that was security's to make) costs five agents' worth of rework, not one. That's the single seat in this project where the highest model ceiling earns its cost. I would not go to xhigh/max for this role: xhigh/max effort spends more test-time compute on deeper deliberation per response, and orchestration work here is mostly reading condensed hand-offs and delegating — breadth of correct judgment across many small decisions, not depth on one hard problem. High is the right ceiling for that shape of work.
12	
13	**Track owners (the five of us, in planning/design mode) — sonnet, medium-to-high, track-dependent.** This is my own judgment, and it's not uniform across tracks — the five tracks are not equally deep:
14	- Product/research framing (01, Naomi) is synthesis and judgment-call writing — Naomi herself recommended sonnet/medium for her own track, and I agree: the value there is scoping and narrative tightness, not deep technical reasoning, so paying for more depth than the task uses is waste.
15	- Security (02, this track) and the engineering track's design decisions (03) involve genuine multi-step reasoning with real correctness stakes — sonnet/high is the right default there.
16	- Data-ops (05, Priya) is the one track where I'd go above sonnet during *planning*, and Priya made the argument better than I would have: the schema and DAO interface contract is precision-heavy but low-volume, and the cost of a wrong nullable-vs-required field or a wrong interface signature ripples into both 03 and 04. That's asymmetric risk, not asymmetric size — one careful opus/high pass on that specific artifact costs little in absolute tokens and avoids a class of downstream rework nothing else in this project can prevent. This is the same "spend the expensive model where a wrong foundational call is costly to reverse" principle I apply to implementation below, just moved one step upstream to where the contract is actually decided.
17	- Infra (04, Theo) explicitly self-assessed low-to-medium, sonnet, and I agree with his reasoning: it's the smallest-surface-area track, it's consumer-not-producer during planning, and over-resourcing it during the planning phase is the same mistake as over-engineering the infra itself.
18	
19	**Throwaway research subagents (fact-gathering, doc lookups, exploring existing code) — haiku, low, or sonnet/low if some judgment is needed.** Naomi's own workflow already does this (haiku/sonnet-low fanned out for industry facts, then one sonnet/medium synthesis pass) and it generalizes: per Anthropic's orchestrator-worker pattern, a subagent's exploration noise never re-enters the coordinator's context — only its condensed output does — so there's no reason to pay for a stronger model on a narrow, verifiable, throwaway task. Fan these out in parallel; that's where cheap-and-fast is unambiguously the right call.
20	
21	**Implementation (writing the actual Go code — DAO, REST handlers, connector) — sonnet, medium-to-high; opus only for one narrow piece.** Renata, who owns this work, put it as sonnet/medium-high: once upstream contracts land, this is CRUD/REST/HTTP-client code against clear specs — not novel enough for opus, but not so trivial that low-effort stubs are acceptable when "reads as intentional engineering" is the grading criterion. Tests (table-driven Go, mocked DB/HTTP) are sonnet/medium. I agree with all of that, and it changes my earlier position: I had planned to reserve opus/high for implementing the DAO's multi-database interface, but if Priya delivers the interface as actual Go signatures (see hand-off structure, §2), the hard decision has already been made upstream at opus/high and implementing against it is sonnet work. That leaves the connector's token lifecycle logic (acquire, cache, refresh, never-persist-in-plaintext) as the one implementation surface where I'd still spend opus/high — the correctness bar is a security property, not just a passing test, and the assignment's question 3 is where a subtle bug most directly contradicts this track's own threat model. Anthropic's own internal eval (research doc §3) found an Opus-lead/Sonnet-subagent configuration beat a single-agent Opus baseline by 90.2% at materially higher token cost; the lesson I take is "spend the expensive model where a wrong foundational call is costly to reverse," not "use Opus everywhere."
22	
23	**Review/verification passes — high effort always; independence scaled to stakes.** Two positions came in here, and they're compatible rather than contradictory. Renata wants a high-effort review pass on her own output before calling it done, same model — catching a busted interface boundary late costs more than the tokens. That's right for engineering surfaces. My addition, Anthropic-motivated but my own extension: their multi-agent paper's rationale for parallel independent subagents is that separation of concerns "reduces path dependency" — an agent reviewing its own output inherits its own blind spots. Resolving the two explicitly: Renata's same-session high-effort self-review is the *first* pass on everything she produces — it's cheap, it catches the interface-boundary class of bug she's worried about, and there's no reason to skip it. For the security-graded surfaces specifically (credential storage design, token handling, the auth/authz scheme, and the connector code that implements them), a *second* pass in a genuinely independent session is added on top, because those are exactly what LoginID said they're grading the reasoning quality of, and a self-review cannot by construction catch an assumption the author didn't know they were making. For everything else (infra sketches, product prose, routine handlers), the self-review alone is proportionate. This is a take-home POC, not a production deploy, and calibrating review independence to actual stakes is part of the "why," not corner-cutting.
24	
25	**Fable reserved narrowly.** I would not spread fable across all five track-owner seats — that's five top-model seats purchased for a take-home whose own cited evidence (the Opus-lead/Sonnet-worker eval) argues for concentrating the strongest model at the lead and running capable-but-cheaper models at the worker tier. The one place I'd consider a second fable seat: a final cross-track consistency pass, run once near submission, checking the five tracks' finished deliverables against each other for contradictions (the exact class of problem Naomi flagged — two tracks independently deciding what `/identity` returns). That's a single, short-lived, high-value use of the top model, not a standing role.
26	
27	## 2. Architecture pattern for planning-phase coordination
28	
29	**Default: orchestrator-worker, not peer-to-peer mesh.** This is Anthropic's own documented pattern (research doc §3 — lead agent decomposes, spawns scoped subagents, subagents report back condensed findings, lead synthesizes) and Naomi independently converged on the same recommendation from the product side: five agents cross-talking as a full mesh during planning is more collision risk than value. Status and scope decisions should route through team-lead.
30	
31	**Exception: direct track-to-track handoffs on a specific shared decision.** A pure hub-and-spoke model is wrong for one case — two tracks with a direct, narrow dependency on each other's specific decision. This track and data-ops already did this, and Priya asked that it be recorded here as the worked example: we coordinated directly on modeling `user_credential.method` as a lookup table rather than a native enum, because that's a two-party decision (her schema, my method-aware credential-storage design), not a project-wide one, and routing it through team-lead both directions would have added latency for no benefit. Renata, by contrast, explicitly doesn't want peer chatter with 01 or 04 — she wants clean hand-offs from 02 and 05 that she reads once and implements against. Both are right; the difference is whether a genuine two-party decision exists. The rule I'd formalize: point-to-point messaging is fine for a specific, bounded, two-track decision; it must still land in a durable file (see §3) as the source of truth, not live only in chat history; and it doesn't substitute for the orchestrator's overall view of status.
32	
33	**Plan mode.** Narrower than I first drafted, after Renata's input. She doesn't want plan mode for her own track — the plan already exists in the assignment text, and if upstream tracks hand her concrete contracts there's nothing left to plan that a plan-mode pass would catch. I think that's correct *conditional on the hand-offs being concrete enough* (see next paragraph); if 05 hands her a prose strategy instead of interface signatures, plan mode before the DAO becomes necessary again because she'd be making design decisions that aren't hers. So the rule: plan mode gates decisions that constrain another track — API contract shapes, schema fields, token formats, the Go interface itself — at the track that owns the decision, before it's declared stable. It does not gate implementation against an already-concrete contract. For a track whose CLAUDE.md brief is already specific and self-contained (per the root file's own stated goal), formal plan-mode approval on top of that brief is redundant — the brief already is the approved plan.
34	
35	**Fork vs. fresh scoped agent.** Fork when a task needs the current session's accumulated context and would lose something real by starting clean (e.g., resuming a security review with everything already threat-modeled in-session). Fresh scoped agent when the task is genuinely independent and carrying forward unrelated context would only dilute it (a documentation lookup, a track that hasn't started). This project's five persistent track-owner teammates are, at a coarser grain, already an instance of "fresh scoped agent": each seeded only with the root `CLAUDE.md` plus its own track file, not all five tracks' accumulated history — that's Anthropic's just-in-time retrieval principle (research doc §2, technique 4) applied at the team-structure level, not just the subagent level.
36	
37	**Hand-off structure.** Per the research doc (§2/§3), Anthropic's own subagents target roughly 1,000–2,000 tokens of condensed findings passed to a coordinator — never a raw transcript. Concretely here: hand-offs happen through each track's `CLAUDE.md` Status section and named deliverable files, referenced by relative path, exactly as the root file already mandates ("cross-reference other tracks by relative path... rather than restating their content"). A `SendMessage` between tracks signals readiness or asks a targeted question — it is not the transport for the actual content, and Renata was explicit that she wants upstream decisions written somewhere durable, not relayed verbally through team-lead.
38	
39	Priya then raised the bar on what "condensed" has to mean for a hand-off into implementation, and I'm adopting her standard project-wide: a hand-off is implementable only if the receiving track can act on it *without re-deriving the sender's reasoning*. For 05→03 that means the actual Go interface signatures (method names, parameter and return types, error semantics), the field-level schema per entity with types and nullability, and explicit notes on what is out of scope for the receiver (e.g., 03 must not invent its own PII/credential separation — that's already decided in 05's governance note). Priya's commitment is to bake that Go-shaped contract directly into `../05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md` so it's copy-pasteable, not descriptive. Prose strategy alone forces re-derivation, which is both wasted tokens and a drift risk. The same standard applies to this track's outbound hand-offs: 02→03 needs the token scheme and where it's validated, not a discussion of alternatives; 02→04 needs, per Theo's ask, an explicit secrets inventory (DB credentials, IDP credentials, signing keys) and a never-log list, because he implements delivery and does not set the requirement. Those two are follow-ups for this track once the planning pause lifts — the existing `api-auth-design.md` and `connector-security.md` contain the decisions but not yet in that receiver-shaped form.
40	
41	The replies I got to my own bounded question were all in the 300–600 word range — the condensed-finding size the research doc describes — which is mild evidence the pattern holds when the question is scoped tightly.
42	
43	**Context and cache management over a multi-hour project.** Two concrete disciplines, both sourced from the research doc's context-engineering section: (1) keep each track's `CLAUDE.md` stable within a working session — edit it at the start or end of a turn, not interleaved mid-task, because an edit to a file currently loaded in context invalidates that cached prefix (research doc §4/§5); (2) when a track's own work legitimately grows long across multiple similar passes (data-ops iterating across DB backends is the clearest case in this project), compact deliberately — write a short settled-decisions summary to a file and start the next pass fresh from that summary rather than carrying the full multi-turn history forward (§2, technique 1). Neither of these is exotic; they're the direct, mechanical application of Anthropic's documented "context rot" mitigation to this project's actual shape of work.
44	
45	**Planning dependency order across tracks.** All five tracks described their dependencies independently and the graph they describe is consistent — nobody claimed a dependency someone else denied. Assembled:
46	
47	1. **First wave, in parallel: product/research (01), security (02), data-ops (05).** None of the three blocks on another. Naomi's framing depends only on the assignment text; my threat model and auth design needed the assignment text plus the one bounded coordination point with 05 (already resolved); Priya states 05 does not block on 01 or 02. Two nuances worth naming. First, Naomi regards 01 as upstream of everyone, while Priya and Renata both say 01 can run "anytime, independently" — those reconcile: nothing *hard*-blocks on 01, but her `api-connector-design-rationale.md` should be designated the single source of truth for field/endpoint shape so engineering isn't re-deriving it, which is a soft dependency (read it before finalizing, not before starting). The assignment text already specifies the question-2/3 endpoint shapes fairly concretely, so the drift risk is real but narrower than a green-field API would carry. Second, 05 is the critical-path member of this wave — it hard-blocks 03 and partially blocks 04 — so if anything in the first wave gets priority attention, it's the schema and interface contract.
48	2. **Engineering (03) has a split start.** Renata drew a useful distinction: 05 is a hard blocker for the DAO code, but 02 is not a blocker for her early scaffolding — project structure, Go module layout, route wiring, stub handlers can begin while the auth decision is still settling, and she needs 02 only before finalizing the middleware. So 03 starts scaffolding as soon as the first wave is underway and starts the DAO the moment 05's contract is stable — "stable" meaning unlikely to be rewritten, not fully polished. Waiting for perfection would idle the track that owns the largest deliverable.
49	3. **Infra (04) is last**, exactly as Theo described his own role: reviewer/consumer during planning (read the other four tracks' files, flag anything infra-shaped so it isn't silently owned elsewhere), producer only once three specific things exist — 03's service topology, config surface, and DAO driver-selection mechanism; 02's secrets inventory and never-log list; 05's migration tool/approach. Building infra design against boundaries that don't exist yet is fiction dressed as a deliverable, in Theo's words, and I don't have a better way to put it.
50	
51	## 3. Where the shared planning artifact lives
52	
53	Three tracks offered a view and they converge. Naomi proposed a single root-level `PLANNING.md` with one section per track, each track editing only its own. Theo wants one owner per section, no shared free-text blob five agents edit concurrently, and suggested a single index file linking to each track's own planning notes — matching the existing directory structure. Renata wants upstream decisions in the owning track's `CLAUDE.md` or a shared design doc she reads once. The common thread is one-owner-per-unit and a thin index, and I'd adopt Theo's shape over Naomi's for two reasons: first, even with an "own section" discipline, five agents live-editing one shared file in a short window is still a collision surface that a table of independent rows avoids more cleanly than prose sections do; second, and more importantly, this project already has a durable decision-record layer — each track's own `CLAUDE.md` plus its deliverable files — and duplicating design content into a second file risks the two drifting apart, which is a worse failure mode than the collision risk it's meant to solve.
54	
55	**Recommendation:** a root-level `PLANNING.md`, purely as a thin cross-track coordination board — one table row per track: status, blocking-on, blocked-by, pointer to that track's planning notes, last-updated — plus, per Theo's specific ask, a single "service boundaries" row or short section that 03 owns and 04 reads, so infra isn't polling engineering's `CLAUDE.md` for updates. Short, cheap to write, cheap to skim, genuinely low collision risk because each row has exactly one writer. The actual reasoning — the "why," which is most of what LoginID is grading — stays where it already lives, in each track's own files, referenced by relative path. This keeps the artifact consistent with the root `CLAUDE.md`'s existing rule (cross-reference, don't restate) instead of introducing a second place the same content has to be kept in sync.
56

1	# Hand-off 02 → 03: Auth scheme, validation point, and authorization requirements
2	
3	Status: **v2** — updated after S8's independent review of `api-auth-design.md` and `connector-security.md` surfaced fixes that affect this hand-off; both already relayed to you by chat, folded into the durable file here per this project's hand-off standard. Receiver: Renata Cole, `../03-engineering-delivery/`. Written so 03 should be able to scaffold and build the middleware without re-deriving the reasoning in `api-auth-design.md`, `connector-security.md`, or the S3/S4/S8 decision records. Full reasoning, if wanted: `api-auth-design.md`, `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`, `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`.
4	
5	## Token scheme
6	
7	- **Grant type:** OAuth2 client-credentials. Machine-to-machine only; no end-user login flow for question 2's API.
8	- **Token format:** JWT, signed, asymmetric (RS256 or ES256 — pick one and use it consistently; RS256 is the safer default if your Go JWT library's ES256 support is less mature).
9	- **Claim set (minimum):** `sub` (calling client identity), `scope` (space-delimited, values below), `exp`, `iat`, `aud` (this API, specifically — reject tokens minted for another audience).
10	- **Literal values, filling the "pending 02's final scheme" placeholder in your Service boundaries config surface:** `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER = "https://auth.loginid-takehome.internal"`, `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE = "loginid-api-service"`. Both are configuration, not secrets — safe in a `ConfigMap` or committed default, per `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3. If `cmd/idp-connector` ever needs its own audience value (it doesn't consume this token scheme today — see "Where it's validated" below), that would be a second, distinct `aud`, not a shared one; not needed for this submission.
11	- **TTL:** 5–15 minutes. No refresh tokens — the client re-authenticates with its own client secret on expiry.
12	- **No denylist.** Revocation is handled by TTL shortness, not a stateful lookup. Don't build one; it's not missing, it's a deliberate trade-off already reasoned through in `api-auth-design.md`.
13	- **Verifier must pin the expected algorithm** (whichever of RS256/ES256 you choose) and reject a token whose header claims any other algorithm, including `alg: none` and a downgrade to a symmetric algorithm using the public key as an HMAC secret (RFC 8725 §3.1). Check your JWT library's default behavior — several popular libraries have shipped this vulnerability by not doing this by default.
14	- **Token-endpoint brute-force protection**, separate from the resource-API rate limits below: failed-grant rate limiting and progressive backoff per `client_id` and per source IP on the token-issuing endpoint itself, plus alerting on a sustained run of failed grants against one `client_id`. This protects the endpoint where a caller presents `client_id`/`client_secret`, which none of the resource-API limits below cover.
15	- **Bearer-token replay is an accepted risk, not a gap to close in implementation.** This token has the same possession-equals-authority property as the connector's vendor token (`connector-security.md`); no PoP/binding is expected or required here. Noted so it isn't mistaken for something to "fix" during implementation.
16	
17	## Where it's validated
18	
19	- **Middleware at the API edge**, in `internal/api/` per your published service boundary — every request to `cmd/api-service` passes through token validation before reaching a handler. `cmd/idp-connector` does not validate *this* token — but per `connector-security.md` §5 (added by S8 review), `idp-connector`'s own `/auth` and `/identity` handlers need the same JWT middleware pattern, with a **distinct audience** (`aud: idp-connector-service`, not `loginid-api-service`) and its own scope (`connector:identity-lookup`), restricted to internal callers (in this project's shape, `api-service` acting on a caller's behalf) — not the same token, not exposed to arbitrary external callers. Two audiences, one mechanism.
20	- **Key retrieval:** middleware verifies with the **public** key only (JWKS or a fetched public key — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3). The private signing key never touches `api-service`; it lives with the token-issuing function, which is a mode of `api-service` (not a separate binary — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` Assumption 2), scoped by RBAC so the verification code path and the issuing code path don't share Secret access even though they're the same binary.
21	- **Key rotation:** expect a `kid` claim; publish old and new public keys together during rotation overlap. Token TTL (5–15 min) bounds how long that overlap needs to last.
22	
23	## Scope vocabulary (final, from S4 — `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`)
24	
25	| Scope | Authorizes | Does not authorize | Object-level check |
26	|---|---|---|---|
27	| `profile:read:own` | Retrieve a single record by id the caller already holds a legitimate reference to (e.g., a partner resolving a referred user) | Search/query by name or phone fragment; retrieval of a record outside the caller's referral relationship | `target.id` checked against `caller_referral` mapping (data owned by `../05-data-ops/`); out-of-set → reject, logged as a policy denial, distinct from an auth failure |
28	| `profile:read:any` | Retrieve a single record by id, no referral constraint | Search/query by fragment | Requires a `reason_code` from a closed enum (below) on every request, logged with the audit record |
29	| `profile:search` | Query by name/phone fragment across the table | — (this is the broadest scope; there is no broader one) | Result cap enforced; a query that would exceed it unconstrained (e.g., bare wildcard) is rejected outright, not truncated |
30	
31	`reason_code` enum for `profile:read:any` (closed, versioned — adding a value is a documented change, not a runtime config toggle): `support_ticket`, `fraud_review`, `kyc_reverification`, `legal_hold`.
32	
33	**Object-level policy hook implementation shape:** a function `authorize(sub, scope, target) -> allow|deny`, called after scope validation, before the handler executes the query/retrieve. This is a second check, not a replacement for the scope check — a valid `profile:read:own` token can still fail `authorize()` if the target isn't in the caller's referral set.
34	
35	## Pagination, rate limits, decomposition resistance (requirements — numbers are fixed, mechanism is yours)
36	
37	- **Cursor-based pagination only** — no offset-based paging. Default page size 20, hard ceiling 50.
38	- **Per-client-credential rate limits:** 60 req/min for `read:own`/`read:any`; 10 req/min for `profile:search`.
39	- **Cumulative distinct-record-touch counter per caller per rolling 24h window**, tracked independently of per-minute rate — this is the control that catches slow, under-the-cap enumeration (decomposition) that per-request and per-minute limits alone miss. **Applies to `profile:read:any` as well as `profile:search`, unconditionally on whether a `reason_code` is present** — a valid, logged `reason_code` satisfies the per-call `authorize()` policy check but does not exempt the caller from this cumulative cap (S8 review fix: the original wording made this counter a no-op for `read:any`, since that scope requires a `reason_code` on every call by design). The counting-store mechanism (Redis, in-process with periodic flush, whatever fits your stack) is your call; the requirement is that it exists, applies to both scopes, and alerts on threshold, not just hard-blocks.
40	- Sustained near-limit usage on any of the above should alert, not just silently throttle.
41	
42	## Audit-log field list (every search/retrieve call)
43	
44	Log these fields, and only these — see `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list for what must never appear here:
45	
46	- `sub` (caller identity)
47	- `scope` used
48	- record id(s) touched (opaque ids, not the PII values)
49	- `reason_code`, when the scope is `profile:read:any`
50	- policy decision (`allow` / `deny`, and if `deny`, whether it was a scope failure or an `authorize()` policy denial — these are distinct signals for detection)
51	- timestamp
52	- request outcome (success/failure/rate-limited)
53	
54	Never in this log: PII field values, full JWTs, full request/response bodies. Per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s new requirement, this log stream itself needs read access distinct from and narrower than whatever can read Secrets — that's 04's mechanism, but don't let the log ship without asking whether that separation exists.
55	
56	## What's not yours to decide (already decided upstream, don't re-derive)
57	
58	- **Grant type, token format, and claim set** — decided in `api-auth-design.md`; not open for re-litigation during implementation.
59	- **Scope vocabulary and provisioning bars** — decided in S4 (`decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`); if a real implementation constraint makes one of these unworkable, flag it back to me rather than adjusting it unilaterally.
60	- **The `DB_DSN_FILE` convention** — per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s S7 revision, this track is asking you to add a file-based alternative to `DB_DSN` (env var becomes a transitional fallback, not a permanent equal option), because a DSN's embedded password is exposed by `kubectl describe`/crash dumps/child-process environment the same as any other secret in that inventory. This is a request into your config surface, not a unilateral change on my part — you decide the exact mechanism (a `_FILE` suffix convention or otherwise), I'm asserting the requirement.
61	- **Password/client-secret hashing** — Argon2id, decided in `threat-model.md` Asset 1; not this hand-off's concern beyond noting it exists.
62	- **PII retention/caching duration for anything the API returns** — `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`'s call, not this track's or yours.
63	- **Whether `read:any` ever gets issued to a real caller** — per S4, the scope stays defined but unissued unless a caller clears the provisioning bar (caller-role table entry, named approver). Don't wire a default grant "just in case."
64	
65	## Open question back to you
66	
67	Nothing blocking. One thing worth a quick confirm when convenient: does your `internal/api/` middleware layer have a natural place to run the object-level `authorize()` check as a distinct step from scope validation (e.g., a second middleware or an explicit call inside each handler before the DAO call), or would you rather this track propose a specific Go interface shape for it? Either is fine — say which you'd find more useful and I'll adjust.
68

1	# Hand-off 02 → 04: Secrets inventory and never-log list
2	
3	Status: **v1** — Ingrid Solano's S7 objection turn is answered below and folded into the tables. Awaiting 04's Newcomer (`wesley-okonkwo`) cold read. Receiver: Theo Bergman, `../04-infra-devops/`. Written to the project's hand-off standard: 04 should be able to act on this without re-deriving the reasoning in `threat-model.md`, `api-auth-design.md`, or `connector-security.md`.
4	
5	## Assumptions this hand-off makes
6	
7	1. Deployment target is the **design-only Kubernetes lab cluster** 04 names (Warren's decision via Theo, `../LOG.md`). Delivery mechanism is therefore Kubernetes-native `Secret` objects unless 04 chooses an external store; every requirement below is written so it still holds if the mechanism changes.
8	2. Service topology is 03's published boundary (`../PLANNING.md`, Service boundaries): two binaries, `cmd/api-service` (Q1 DAO + Q2 REST API) and `cmd/idp-connector` (Q3), env-var configuration only. Variable names below are 03's; two are marked as pending this track's final scheme. **The token-issuing authorization-server function is a mode of `api-service`, not a third binary** — this take-home's scope doesn't justify a standalone authz server, and 03 has published no third `cmd/` entrypoint. It runs under `api-service`'s own service account; RBAC in row #2 is scoped to that account, restricted (via a distinct Kubernetes Role, not a separate ServiceAccount) so that only the issuing code path — not the token-verification code path in the same binary — can `get` the signing-key Secret. If 03 later decides to split this out as its own binary, this row's RBAC target changes with it; flag that to me if it happens, don't silently re-derive it in 04.
9	3. An **external operator agent** — a human or automated actor outside this project, holding cluster-admin or namespace-admin rights on 04's design-only lab cluster, who applies Kubernetes manifests to it — is a privileged actor outside this service: anyone who can apply a manifest can read any Secret it mounts. Consequence for 04: manifests reference Secrets by name and never carry values; values are set out-of-band. This is a named trust boundary in `threat-model.md`, not a silent assumption; nothing further about who that operator is needs to be re-derived from 04's own files to act on this hand-off.
10	4. Nothing is deployed and nobody contacts the cluster or its operator during the take-home. This document describes what *would* be delivered, not what has been.
11	
12	## Secrets inventory
13	
14	| # | Secret | Used by | Injection requirement (02 sets it) | Rotation expectation | Kubernetes-native note (04's mechanism) | Never-log |
15	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
16	| 1 | Database credentials, carried inside `DB_DSN` | `api-service` | Runtime-injected; never in source, image, or committed config. **Required, not preferred: file mount via a `DB_DSN_FILE` convention** (03 to add alongside `DB_DSN`), because a DSN's embedded password is exposed by `kubectl describe`, crash dumps, and child-process environment exactly as much as any other secret in this table — there is no citation that makes `DB_DSN` a lesser case, so this track is not treating it as one | On credential rotation; service restart or reload acceptable | `Secret` mounted read-only as a file, read via `DB_DSN_FILE`; env-var `DB_DSN` is a fallback only until 03 ships the file-based convention, not a permanent equal option | Yes — the DSN contains the password; log the driver name and host at most |
17	| 2 | JWT signing **private** key (RS256/ES256), per `api-auth-design.md` | Token-issuing authorization-server component only — **not** `api-service`, which verifies with the public key | Runtime-injected as a **file**, never an env var; asymmetric so the API never holds it | Key rotation with a `kid` claim; publish old and new public keys together during overlap; TTL of tokens (5–15 min) bounds the overlap window | `Secret` mounted read-only as a file; restrict `get`/`list` on this Secret to the issuer's service account via RBAC | Yes |
18	| 3 | JWT **public** key / JWKS | `api-service` middleware | Not a secret; may ship in config or be fetched from the issuer | Follows #2 | Plain `ConfigMap` or fetched URL; listed here so 04 does not over-protect it | No (but never log full tokens — see below) |
19	| 4 | API caller `client_secret`s (OAuth2 client-credentials) | Authorization server (verifies); callers hold their own copy | **Stored hashed** (Argon2id, same floor as `threat-model.md` Asset 1), never plaintext at rest; issued once at provisioning and not recoverable | Per-client rotation on demand; revoke by deleting the hash | Lives in the issuer's datastore, not a Kubernetes `Secret`; 04 delivers only that datastore's credentials (#1-shaped) | Yes |
20	| 5 | Connector's own vendor registration: `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_ID`, `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_SECRET` (and the XYZ pair if a second vendor is configured) | `idp-connector` | Runtime-injected; never in source or committed config; `client_id` is low-sensitivity but travels with the secret and gets the same handling | Per vendor's policy; expect manual rotation | One `Secret` per vendor so rotating ABC never touches XYZ; file mount preferred | Yes for the secret; keep the id out of logs too |
21	| 6 | Vendor `access_token` (obtained via `/auth`, used for `/identity`) | `idp-connector`, in memory | **Not a deployed secret** — transient. If cached at all: TTL ≤ vendor expiry, encrypted at rest with #7, keyed per vendor account (`connector-security.md` §1) | Expires on its own; never persisted across restarts | No `Secret` object; if 04 provisions a cache, it must be encrypted using #7 and reachable only from `idp-connector` | Yes — never, including error paths |
22	| 7 | Data-encryption key (KEK) for envelope encryption of any TOTP shared secrets and any cached vendor tokens | `api-service` (TOTP) — **not a committed feature of this submission**; `user_credential.method` is modeled as an extensible lookup table (`../05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md`) that supports TOTP as a *possible* value, but no TOTP implementation is planned unless 03 builds one. `idp-connector` (token cache) — **also not committed**: S3's red/blue on the connector token lifecycle (in progress) is deciding whether the connector caches vendor tokens at all; current direction (Felix Adebayo's alternatives turn) is "no cache by default, fetched fresh per `/identity` call," which would remove this use case too. This row is provisioned only if either feature materializes — it is a contingency the inventory names in advance, not a live requirement for 04 to build now | Runtime-injected as a file; the KEK never leaves the process that decrypts | Rotation by re-wrapping DEKs; never requires re-hashing passwords (those are hashed, not encrypted) | `Secret` (file mount) or an external KMS if 04 prefers — the requirement is only that a KEK exists and is not in source | Yes |
23	| 7a | Credential to reach the external KMS, **only if 04 exercises the KMS alternative in row #7** (a role, API key, or client cert — whichever the chosen KMS uses) | Whichever binary holds the KEK reference (#7) | Same discipline as every other row here: runtime-injected, never in source, never provisioned ad hoc outside this table | Per the KMS provider's own rotation mechanism | Cloud-KMS access is typically workload-identity-based (no static credential to store) rather than a `Secret` object; if the chosen KMS instead requires a static credential, it gets its own `Secret` under the same RBAC discipline as row #2 | Yes |
24	| 8 | TLS serving private keys for `api-service` and `idp-connector` | Ingress / each binary, per 04's termination choice | Runtime-injected; TLS 1.2+ only; certificate validation never disabled in any environment (`connector-security.md` §2) | Per certificate lifetime; automated issuance preferred | `kubernetes.io/tls` `Secret`; issuance mechanism is 04's call | Yes |
25	| 9 | End-user's vendor password (`POST /auth` request body) | `idp-connector`, transient | Never persisted, never cached, held only for the vendor call | n/a | Nothing for 04 to deliver; listed so nobody creates a place for it | **Yes — under no circumstance, including stack traces** |
26	
27	Names marked pending in 03's boundary: `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER` / `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` are configuration, not secrets; this track will confirm their final values in `handoff-03-auth.md`. They are listed here only so 04 does not classify them as secrets.
28	
29	## Kubernetes-specific requirements (04 implements; 02 sets)
30	
31	- **Secrets at rest in etcd are base64, not encrypted, by default.** Either enable encryption at rest for the `Secret` resource type, or source values from an external store that never writes plaintext to etcd. One or the other; the design must say which.
32	- **RBAC:** no service account may `get`/`list` Secrets it does not mount. The operator agent's apply rights are a documented trust boundary, not a hole to close in this take-home — but the design should note that its scope could be narrowed to a namespace.
33	- **Manifests carry references, never values.** Any manifest in the repository contains Secret *names*; values are provided out-of-band. This is what keeps assumption 3 honest.
34	- **Prefer file mounts over env vars** for #2, #5, #7, #8. #1 is env-var only because 03's config surface is env-var only; if 03 accepts a `_FILE` suffix convention, move it.
35	- **No secret in an image layer, build arg, or CI log.** CI redaction is 04's mechanism; the requirement is that a build log can be published without exposing anything in this table.
36	- **Audit/access logs (the output of the never-log list below) are readable only by an incident-review role, distinct from and narrower than general operator access.** `sub`, scope, record id, and timestamp are enough on their own to reconstruct who looked up whom — that log stream needs its own read restriction, separate from whichever service account can `get`/`list` the Secrets in this table. Mechanism (log-pipeline RBAC, a separate log sink with its own access control, etc.) is 04's call; the requirement is that "can read Secrets" and "can read the audit trail" are not the same grant.
37	
38	## Never-log list (applies to every component, every environment, every log level, including error paths and stack traces)
39	
40	1. End-user vendor password (#9).
41	2. Vendor `access_token` (#6) — and any `Authorization` header value, inbound or outbound, in full.
42	3. PII field values from `user_profile` or from `/identity` responses: name, phone, `street_address`, `locality`, `region`, `postal_code`, `country`. Log the *fact* of access — caller `sub`, scope used, opaque record id, timestamp, status — never the values.
43	4. Raw vendor error response bodies (they can echo submitted PII or partial credentials). Log status code and vendor error code only.
44	5. JWT signing private key (#2) and any JWT in full — log `sub`, `aud`, `exp`, and `kid` if needed, never the token.
45	6. API caller `client_secret`s (#4), in plaintext or hashed.
46	7. `DB_DSN` (#1) or any connection string containing credentials.
47	8. Full request bodies of `POST /auth` and `POST /identity` — both contain items above by construction.
48	9. Search query parameters containing a name or phone fragment (`api-auth-design.md`, hardening) — they are PII; redact or hash before any access log, including proxy and ingress logs 04 controls.
49	
50	## What 04 does not decide (already decided upstream)
51	
52	Token TTLs and claim set (`api-auth-design.md`); scope vocabulary (`handoff-03-auth.md`, pending); password and client-secret hashing (`threat-model.md` Asset 1); whether the connector caches vendor tokens (`connector-security.md` §1 — it may, under the conditions in #6); PII retention windows (`../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`).
53	
54	## S7 objection turn — Ingrid Solano, resolved
55	
56	All four objections accepted; no decline.
57	
58	1. **Authorization-server ownership gap.** Accepted. Assumption 2 now states the authz-issuing function is a mode of `api-service`, scoped by a Role narrower than the token-verification code path in the same binary — not a phantom third deployable.
59	2. **Missing KMS-access-credential row.** Accepted. Added row #7a with the same columns as every other row, rather than leaving it as a footnote inside #7.
60	3. **Env-var exception for `DB_DSN`.** Accepted — the objection is correct that "prefer, if 03 allows" was this track declining a call it owns. Upgraded to a requirement: a `DB_DSN_FILE` convention, with the env var as a transitional fallback only. Carried into `handoff-03-auth.md` as an ask to 03.
61	4. **No RBAC on audit-log read access.** Accepted. Added a requirement under Kubernetes-specific requirements that Secret-read and audit-log-read are distinct grants.
62
