1	# UX Notes — Two Surfaces on Top of This Backend
2	
3	Prepared for the `01-product-industry-research-design` track. See `../CLAUDE.md` for the assignment and `./CLAUDE.md` for this track's scope: this is light-touch, backend-supporting narrative, not a design deliverable in its own right — no wireframes, no design canvas. Text, one page, describing screens and the one design rule each surface enforces.
4	
5	## Surface 1: the admin profile-search console (Persona 2)
6	
7	**Who uses it:** the support/ops analyst from `personas-use-cases.md`, resolving a ticket or investigating a flagged transaction.
8	
9	**Screens:**
10	
11	- **Search screen.** A single form: name, phone, partial-match toggle. No blank-query submit — the form requires at least one field filled before the search button is enabled, matching the "no unconstrained query" product requirement.
12	- **Results list.** Each row shows masked/partial PII by default (e.g., a partially redacted phone number, a name, no address) and an "expand" affordance per row, not a bulk-select checkbox anywhere on the screen.
13	- **Expanded record view.** Full PII for one record, reached only through the per-row expand action. This is a different screen state, not a hover-to-reveal or an inline toggle on the results list — the extra navigation step is deliberate and consistent with the backend treating expanded access as a distinct, auditable event.
14	
15	**The one design rule this surface enforces:** masked-by-default, expand-is-a-distinct-action. Every other screen decision — no bulk-select, expand requiring its own navigation step, no "export" button anywhere — is downstream of that one rule, not a separate design choice each time.
16	
17	## Surface 2: the onboarding pre-fill flow (Persona 3)
18	
19	**Who uses it:** a new user signing up for a service that offers "sign in with a partner provider" instead of a blank registration form, per Persona 3 in `personas-use-cases.md`.
20	
21	**Screens:**
22	
23	- **Provider choice.** A short list of partner providers (ABC, XYZ, ...), each a single button — the user picks one, consistent with the connector being provider-agnostic on the backend.
24	- **Partner credential entry.** Username/password fields, framed as "sign in to `<provider>`," not the service's own login form — visually distinct, to signal whose credential this is.
25	- **Pre-filled review screen.** Name, phone, and address arrive already filled in from the `/identity` response; the user reviews and confirms rather than retyping. Fields are editable, not read-only — the provider is a starting point, not an irrevocable source of truth for this signup.
26	
27	**The one design rule this surface enforces:** the partner's password is never visible to, or stored by, this service past the token exchange — the review screen never displays a password field, partner or otherwise, and nothing on this screen persists the credential entered on the previous one. That is the UI-level expression of the connector's `/auth` → `/identity` two-step: a short-lived token stands in for the password everywhere past the first screen.

47	=== cursor/offset ===
48	02-ai-security-architecture/api-auth-design.md:59:- **Cursor-based pagination**, not offset — offset walks turn a capped page size into full-table enumeration. Default page 20, hard ceiling 50.
49	02-ai-security-architecture/handoff-03-auth.md:37:- **Cursor-based pagination only** — no offset-based paging. Default page size 20, hard ceiling 50.
50	02-ai-security-architecture/decisions/search-authz-scoping.md:20:**Pagination/rate limits:** cursor-based (not offset), default page 20 / ceiling 50; rate limits 60 req/min for `read:own`/`read:any`, 10 req/min for `search`, with alerting on sustained near-limit usage.
51	05-data-ops/PLAN.md:34:The one genuinely open design question in the contract, and the one with the most ways to be quietly wrong. Decides: the `ProfileQuery` field set and which fields are optional; match semantics per field (name partial/fuzzy, phone exact-or-prefix on the E.164-normalized value, region/country exact); how empty and multiple filters combine (AND, and whether a wholly empty query is a legal "list everything" or an error); pagination as limit/offset versus a keyset cursor, with a maximum page size; sort order and whether it is stable enough for offset pagination to be meaningful at all; and what `total` means — total matching rows, or rows on this page. Also settles how the documented SQLite parity gap surfaces to a caller: identical results with worse performance, or ranked-vs-unranked differences a caller could observe.
52	05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md:191:    Offset  int     // 0..10_000
53	05-data-ops/decisions/search-query-shape.md:20:    Offset  int     // 0..10_000
54	05-data-ops/decisions/search-query-shape.md:36:- **Pagination.** Limit/offset. Max page 100, default 50, offset ceiling 10 000. `Limit < 0` or `> 100`, and `Offset < 0` or `> 10_000`, return `ErrInvalidQuery`. Keyset rejected as designing for an uncommitted dataset size. Stated cost: offset pages are unstable under concurrent inserts — a row can shift or repeat across pages.
55	05-data-ops/decisions/search-query-shape.md:37:- **`total`.** Exact count of all matching rows, ignoring limit/offset, via a second `COUNT(*)` in no wrapping transaction, so it may disagree with the page by a row under concurrent writes. **`total` is a magnitude, not a promise of reachability** — it can exceed what the offset ceiling allows a caller to page to.
56	05-data-ops/decisions/search-query-shape.md:65:| 5 | `total` can report a count the offset ceiling forbids reaching | **Accepted** — `total` restated as a magnitude |
57	
58	=== phone partial/prefix ===
59	05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md:9:- **`ProfileRepository`** — `Get(ctx, id) (*UserProfile, error)`; `Search(ctx, query) (results []UserProfile, total int, error)` where `query` filters by name (partial/fuzzy), phone (exact/prefix), region, country, plus limit/offset for pagination; `Create(ctx, *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)`; `Update(ctx, *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)` implemented as a portable upsert; `Delete(ctx, id) error`.
60	05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md:187:    Phone   *string // EXACT match on the E.164-normalized value (not prefix)
61	05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md:220:**`Update` and `Upsert` are FULL REPLACEMENTS, not partial patches.** This is the single most consequential thing in this document and it was missing from the first draft. Every column is written from the struct you pass. **A nil `Phone` sets the stored phone to NULL; it does not leave the existing value alone.** A caller wanting patch semantics must read, modify, and write back. Stated this loudly because both readings are reasonable and the wrong guess silently destroys data.
62	05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md:413:| B-Tree on `phone` | all | `Search` phone filter. **Exact match only** — the `ProfileQuery.Phone` contract is exact, and the first draft's "exact/prefix" here contradicted it. The index would support prefix matching; the contract does not offer it. |
63	05-data-ops/PLAN.md:34:The one genuinely open design question in the contract, and the one with the most ways to be quietly wrong. Decides: the `ProfileQuery` field set and which fields are optional; match semantics per field (name partial/fuzzy, phone exact-or-prefix on the E.164-normalized value, region/country exact); how empty and multiple filters combine (AND, and whether a wholly empty query is a legal "list everything" or an error); pagination as limit/offset versus a keyset cursor, with a maximum page size; sort order and whether it is stable enough for offset pagination to be meaningful at all; and what `total` means — total matching rows, or rows on this page. Also settles how the documented SQLite parity gap surfaces to a caller: identical results with worse performance, or ranked-vs-unranked differences a caller could observe.
64	05-data-ops/decisions/search-query-shape.md:16:    Phone   *string // optional: exact match on the E.164-normalized value
65	05-data-ops/decisions/handoff-legibility-05-03.md:23:1. **`Update`: full replace or partial patch — undefined.** Nothing in the contract answered whether passing a profile with `Phone` nil NULLs the stored column or leaves it. Both readings are defensible; the wrong guess silently destroys data. *Ruled: full replacement, stated in bold. Patch semantics are read-modify-write at the caller.* She called this the single most consequential gap and was right.
66	05-data-ops/decisions/handoff-legibility-05-03.md:37:`Create` and the `ID` field contradicted each other (§2 said the DAO generates it, §3 returned `ErrAlreadyExists` "if the ID is taken") — ruled: caller passes `""`, non-empty is `ErrInvalidArgument`. Ownership of `CreatedAt`/`UpdatedAt` unstated — ruled: DAO-owned, ignored on input. `total` never defined in the contract despite `PLAN.md` naming it as something S2 had to settle — the intent-versus-text gap; now defined as all matching rows ignoring limit/offset, and as a magnitude rather than a promise of reachability. `Phone` match semantics contradicted between the struct comment ("exact") and the index table ("exact/prefix") — ruled exact. All-nil `ProfileQuery` legality unstated — ruled legal. `Delete` on a missing row unstated — ruled `ErrNotFound`. `GetByUsername` case-folding ownership unstated — ruled DAO-side. The SQLite `GLOB` phone constraint was left as "a GLOB equivalent" — she correctly noted that inventing it is a design decision, not an implementation detail; it is now given in full.

   1 # Track 04 — Infra & DevOps Backlog
   2 
   3 Track: 04-infra-devops. Lead: Theo Bergman. Epic: "Infra & DevOps: Containerization, CI/CD, and Operations" (key: TBD, team-lead fills in on transcription).
   4 
   5 Authored now per Phase 4; will be re-checked against the consistency pass's cross-cutting findings (`PLAN.md` §"Cross-cutting — enforcement sites") once that report lands, particularly for Story 6 below. All stories carry `no-code-yet` — Warren's Phase 4 go covers backlog authoring, not implementation; a separate, explicit code go-ahead is still required before any `.go`, Dockerfile, CI config, or compose file is written for real.
   6 
   7 ## Story 1 — Containerize `api-service` and `idp-connector`
   8 
   9 **Description:** Build the two multi-stage Go images (distroless final stage, non-root, read-only root filesystem) and the Deployment/Service/NetworkPolicy manifests targeting the lab cluster, per `./containerization-design.md`. Covers both binaries' Dockerfiles, resource requests/limits sized per service (CPU-bound `api-service` vs. I/O-bound `idp-connector`), the migration `Job` (parallelism 1, gated by the CI pipeline's exclusivity mechanism, not Kubernetes ordering alone), and a default-deny `NetworkPolicy` on `idp-connector` so its "no inbound Service" isolation claim actually holds against the namespace's default-allow network.
  10 
  11 **Acceptance criteria:**
  12 - [ ] The receiving track (whoever implements/reviews this) can act on the manifests without re-deriving the reasoning in `containerization-design.md`.
  13 - [ ] Both images build from a pinned Go version, produce a static binary, and contain nothing but the binary (+ CA certs for `idp-connector`) — enforcement site: multi-stage Dockerfile's final `COPY` list.
  14 - [ ] Neither Pod can write to its own root filesystem at runtime — enforcement site: `securityContext.readOnlyRootFilesystem: true`.
  15 - [ ] `idp-connector` is unreachable except from `api-service` — enforcement site: the `NetworkPolicy` ingress rule scoped to `api-service`'s pod selector.
  16 - [ ] A stuck `idp-connector` cannot starve `api-service` if colocated on one node — enforcement site: `resources.limits` on both Pod specs.
  17 - [ ] The migration `Job` cannot start a second concurrent run — enforcement site: the CI pipeline's concurrency-gated deploy stage (Story 3), not a database lock.
  18 
  19 **Depends on:** 03 — `cmd/api-service` and `cmd/idp-connector` build as real binaries (service boundaries already stable, per `PLANNING.md`); 05 — final migration tooling name for the `Job`'s invocation (already final, `05-data-ops/migration-approach.md`).
  20 
  21 **Model/effort:** Sonnet, medium.
  22 
  23 **Type:** Story.
  24 
  25 **Labels:** `track-04`, `no-code-yet`.
  26 
  27 ## Story 2 — Deliver secrets to both services per 02's inventory
  28 
  29 **Description:** Implement the Kubernetes-native `Secret` delivery mechanism in `./secrets-delivery.md`: file-mounted secrets keyed by name only in manifests, RBAC scoped per-service (and, for the JWT signing key, scoped to the issuing code path specifically, not `api-service`'s verification path), cluster-level encryption-at-rest for the `Secret` resource type, and the migration-runner credential's backend-specific provisioning (`CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm` rights on PostgreSQL only, nothing extra on CockroachDB, no privilege model on SQLite) kept distinct from the runtime service credential (DML-only, no DDL, per 05's R4).
  30 
  31 **Acceptance criteria:**
  32 - [ ] 02 (the hand-off's author) confirms this implementation matches `handoff-04-secrets.md` v2's inventory without needing to re-explain any row.
  33 - [ ] No `Secret` value appears in a manifest, image layer, build arg, or CI log — enforcement site: manifests reference names only; CI's `gitleaks` stage (Story 3).
  34 - [ ] No ServiceAccount can `get`/`list` a `Secret` it doesn't mount — enforcement site: per-service `Role`/`RoleBinding` scoped to named `Secret`s.
  35 - [ ] The migration-runner credential and the runtime service credential are provisioned as two distinct `Secret`s, never one — enforcement site: separate `Secret` objects, separate `Role`s, per 05's R4.
  36 - [ ] `Secret` resources are encrypted at rest in etcd — enforcement site: cluster `EncryptionConfiguration`.
  37 
  38 **Depends on:** 02 — `handoff-04-secrets.md` (received, v2, final); 05 — `migration-approach.md` R4 (final).
  39 
  40 **Model/effort:** Sonnet, medium.
  41 
  42 **Type:** Story.
  43 
  44 **Labels:** `track-04`, `security-graded`, `no-code-yet`.
  45 
  46 ## Story 3 — CI/CD pipeline: build, lint, test, scan, migrate, describe-only deploy
  47 
  48 **Description:** Stand up the pipeline in `./ci-pipeline.md`: build/lint/test(`-race`, three DB targets: SQLite, Postgres, CockroachDB)/security-scan/image-build on every PR; push-images/migration-job/describe-only-deploy on merge. The migration-job stage is where 05's R2 exclusivity requirement is actually enforced — a concurrency-gated pipeline stage, not a database lock, since no single lock mechanism covers both the right scope (session, to span `goose`'s per-file transactions) and the right engine (CockroachDB supports transaction-scope only).
  49 
  50 **Acceptance criteria:**
  51 - [ ] A developer or reviewer can act on this pipeline description without re-deriving why each stage exists.
  52 - [ ] A lint or test failure blocks merge — enforcement site: required-status-check on the PR, not advisory.
  53 - [ ] A CockroachDB-specific SQL incompatibility surfaces at PR time, not deploy time — enforcement site: the dedicated CockroachDB testcontainer job.
  54 - [ ] Only one migration run can reach the migrate stage at a time, across concurrent deploys — enforcement site: the pipeline's own concurrency group/mutex on the deploy environment.
  55 - [ ] No secret enters a build log or image layer — enforcement site: `gitleaks` stage plus the Dockerfile's minimal `COPY` list (Story 1).
  56 - [ ] The final deploy stage is documented (which manifests, which namespace, which credential) but not invoked — enforcement site: stage exists as a named, non-executing step until a separate real-deploy decision is made.
  57 
  58 **Depends on:** 03 — test-strategy outline for `go test ./...` to run against; 05 — `migration-approach.md` (final).
  59 
  60 **Model/effort:** Sonnet, medium.
  61 
  62 **Type:** Story.
  63 
  64 **Labels:** `track-04`, `no-code-yet`.

  84 ## Story 5 — Observability: logging, metrics, never-log enforcement, retention-sweep alerting
  85 
  86 **Description:** Implement `./observability.md`: structured `slog` logging with an explicit-field discipline (never a whole request/response struct through a log call), a lint check flagging never-log-list field names, RED metrics with no PII-cardinality labels, ingress-level query-string redaction for search endpoints, RBAC-separated audit-log read access, and 05's S5 retention-sweep requirement — rows-examined/rows-deleted/oldest-surviving-row-age metrics per data class, with an alert when oldest-row-age exceeds retention-window-plus-sweep-interval, since a dead sweep job produces zero rows-deleted either way and gives no other symptom.
  87

  91 - [ ] Search-endpoint name/phone query fragments never appear in ingress access logs — enforcement site: ingress log-format override / redaction.
  92 - [ ] Audit-log read access is a distinct RBAC grant from Secret-read access — enforcement site: separate `Role` bound to an incident-review group.
  93 - [ ] A stopped retention-sweep job triggers an alert within one sweep interval of its window being exceeded — enforcement site: the `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds` alert rule, per data class.

Classified by the most severe finding in each: **9 CONTRADICTION** (seams 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10, 11), **5 GAP** (seams 6, 9, 12, vocabulary sweep, deletion gap), **0 clean AGREE** — though the core test in eight seams passes (endpoint shapes, scope vocabulary, sentinel set, DB_DSN_FILE precedence, issuer/audience literals, R1–R5, retention numbers, §6 enforcement sites), and most contradictions are one-sentence stale references left behind by a later ruling. Fifty individual findings are numbered F1–F50 below.\n\nThe three most consequential: **(1) the vendor token has no consistent home** — 01's onboarding flow holds it across two screens (`ux-notes.md:27`), the assignment's `/auth` returns it to the caller, yet 02 says it is never returned to callers and lives inside one function call (`connector-security.md:19-23`); no document says what our `/auth` returns or how `/identity` obtains vendor authority (F3). **(2) The JWT signing key is mounted into every `api-service` replica** (`containerization-design.md:55,61-62`) while 02 requires it never touch `api-service` and at the same time defines the issuer as \"a mode of `api-service`\" (`handoff-04-secrets.md:8,17`); Secret-`get` RBAC does nothing for a mounted volume (F13). **(3) The final migration layout puts all `CREATE TABLE`s in a `shared/` series** (`migration-approach.md:24`) that the field-level schema cannot express — every table has per-engine types, collations and CHECK syntax (`multi-db-strategy.md:341-368`) — and the document still carries an \"under review in story S1\" item after S1 closed (F28).\n\nAlso material: cursor-only pagination in 02's hand-off versus `Offset int` in 05's accepted contract (F-pag); partial phone match required by 01, exact-only in 05 (F9); no deletion-job or `deletion_log` method in the DAO contract, so the retention mechanism cannot be written against it (F21); the never-log list does not cover the single most likely PII-to-log path — Postgres CHECK/unique-violation `DETAIL` lines carry the failing row (F50); and 02's AI-workflow narrative miscounts its own red/blue tally against the decision record it cites (F36).\n\n**Verdict:** the submission is not internally consistent enough to declare planning done as it stands. It will be after bounded two-party fixes on F3, F13, F28, F-pag, F21 and F50, plus the stale-status sweep in seam 11 — none of which requires reopening a debate, and each of which names one document to change. Re-run seams 1/4, 3, 5, 7 and 9 after those land.\n\n## Seam 1 — `/identity` response and `/auth` request shapes — CONTRADICTION\n\n**F1 (agree).** The shapes are identical everywhere they appear: root `CLAUDE.md` assignment text; `01/api-connector-design-rationale.md:71` (\"Endpoints, methods, and JSON bodies for `/auth` and `/identity` are fixed by the assignment\") and `:73` (the seven PII fields \"match the assignment exactly\"); `02/connector-security.md:7-8`; `03/PLAN.md:23` (\"per the assignment's fixed JSON contracts\"); `05/pii-governance.md:7` lists the same five address subfields; `05/multi-db-strategy.md:115-122` carries them as columns. Nobody drifted on the fixed contract.\n\n**F2 (contradiction — 01 vs 02).** 01's contract summary tells 03 to build `Authenticate(username, password) → token` and `FetchIdentity(token, phone, name) → PII` with \"the calling code (onboarding flow)\" using both (`api-connector-design-rationale.md:40,72`) — the token is returned from one method to the caller and passed into the next. 02 requires the opposite: the token \"is not passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function\" and its scope \"runs from the `/auth` response to the immediately following `/identity` call and no further\" (`connector-security.md:20`). 03 receives both as \"build against this\". Owner: Naomi. Smallest fix, one document (`api-connector-design-rationale.md` contract summary): state that the two-method shape is the vendor adapter's internal interface and the exported operation is a single identity lookup that performs the auth-then-fetch inside the adapter, so the token never crosses the adapter boundary.\n\n**F3 (contradiction — 01, 02, assignment).** The documents cannot all be true about who holds the vendor token between our `/auth` and our `/identity`. The assignment says `/auth` \"retrieves an `access_token`\" for its caller. 01 builds on that: \"a short-lived token stands in for the password everywhere past the first screen\" (`ux-notes.md:27`), i.e., held across two user-facing requests; `personas-use-cases.md:33` says the same. 02 says the token is \"fetched fresh from `/auth` for each `/identity` call, held only inside that single request's execution scope\" (`connector-security.md:19`), is \"Not returned to our own API callers\" (`:23`), and the end-user's vendor password is \"Never persisted, never cached, held only for the vendor call\" (`handoff-04-secrets.md:25`). Our `/identity` body is fixed as `{\"phone\",\"name\"}` with no credential field. So on a `/identity` call the connector has neither a cached token, nor the user's password, nor a token from the caller — unless the caller presents one, which `:23` forbids. `connector-security.md` §6 (`:58-60`) discusses \"a vendor `access_token` obtained from one person's `/auth` call\" being used at `/identity`, which presumes exactly the caller-presented flow `:23` rules out. This is the ambiguity Ingrid's S8 finding 2 (`decisions/second-review-security-docs.md:33`) brushed against without resolving. Owner: Marcus (01's UX follows the assignment; 02's restriction is the added constraint). Smallest fix, one document (`connector-security.md` §1): one paragraph stating what our `/auth` returns to its authenticated internal caller (the vendor token pass-through, or an opaque handle) and that our `/identity` accepts it as the vendor credential for that call; rewrite `:23` from \"not returned to our own API callers\" to \"returned only to the authenticated `connector:identity-lookup` caller, never to end users, never persisted server-side\". If 02 instead keeps `:23`, then the assignment's `/auth` has no purpose in this design and 01's two-screen flow must be withdrawn — which is why the fix belongs in 02.\n\n## Seam 2 — Authorization scopes and their semantics — CONTRADICTION\n\n**F4 (agree).** The scope vocabulary is identical in `02/api-auth-design.md:38-40`, `02/handoff-03-auth.md:25-31`, and `02/decisions/search-authz-scoping.md:14`; the closed `reason_code` enum matches (`api-auth-design.md:54` ↔ `handoff-03-auth.md:31`); the `profile:read:any` decomposition-counter fix is present in both `api-auth-design.md:62` and `handoff-03-auth.md:39` with the same wording and the same S8 attribution. 01's product requirements (`personas-use-cases.md:39-54`) are honoured on attribution, pagination, no-bulk-export and volume limiting.\n\n**F5 (contradiction — internal to `handoff-03-auth.md`).** Line 10: \"If `cmd/idp-connector` ever needs its own audience value (it doesn't consume this token scheme today …) … not needed for this submission.\" Line 19: \"`idp-connector`'s own `/auth` and `/identity` handlers need the same JWT middleware pattern, with a **distinct audience** (`aud: idp-connector-service` …) … Two audiences, one mechanism.\" The v2 update added line 19 without removing line 10. Owner: Marcus. 1	# IDP Connector Security Design — Question 3
2	
3	Owner: Marcus Ilori (`02-ai-security-architecture`). Covers the third-party identity-provider connector (generic vendors "ABC"/"XYZ") exposing `/auth` and `/identity`, per the assignment. Implementation target: `../03-engineering-delivery/CLAUDE.md`.
4	
5	## Contract recap (from the assignment, verbatim in `../CLAUDE.md`)
6	
7	- `POST /auth` — body `{"username": "<string>", "password": "<string>"}` → returns an `access_token`.
8	- `POST /identity` — body `{"phone": "<string>", "name": "<string>"}` → returns PII (name, phone, address fields).
9	
10	Read literally, this connector is a thin proxy in front of ABC/XYZ: it takes vendor credentials, gets a vendor token, and uses that token to pull PII from the vendor. Every security question here is "what does this service do with a vendor secret and vendor PII while it's in our custody," since we don't control the vendor's own security posture.
11	
12	## Threat surface specific to this connector
13	
14	This extends the third-party-token row in `threat-model.md`; this document is the concrete design against those threats.
15	
16	### 1. What happens to the vendor `access_token` once retrieved
17	
18	- **Never logged.** The `/auth` request body contains a vendor password; the response contains a bearer token. A naive "log the request and response for debugging" habit puts both in the same log line — this is called out explicitly because it's the single most common way a connector like this leaks a credential in practice, not a hypothetical.
19	- **No persistent or cached token, by default.** Revised by the S3 red/blue run (`decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`): the token is fetched fresh from `/auth` for each `/identity` call, held only inside that single request's execution scope, and discarded when the call returns — not written to any cache or datastore as a matter of course. Caching is an explicitly-justified exception requiring its own threat re-review if latency or vendor rate limits ever force the question; if that exception is ever opened, it must satisfy: (a) cache key derived from an immutable, vendor-issued identifier (our tenant id + vendor id + the vendor's own opaque subject id) — never from request-supplied `name`/`phone`, which are attacker-influenced; (b) TTL no longer than the vendor's own token expiry, encrypted at rest (envelope encryption via KMS, mechanics owned by `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`); (c) if the KMS/envelope key is unreachable at write or read time, fail closed to "no cache, fetch live" for that call — never to an unencrypted write and never to a hard connector failure.
20	- **Fetch-use-zeroize boundary, stated explicitly so it survives implementation:** the token variable's scope runs from the `/auth` response to the immediately following `/identity` call and no further; it is not passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function, and it is not reused across a retry. **A retried `/identity` call re-fetches via `/auth`; it never reuses a token held from a prior attempt** — otherwise the "one request" exposure window `§4`'s retry/backoff allowance quietly becomes "however long the retry policy runs." Go gives no guaranteed memory zeroing without unsafe/cgo, so a crash dump taken mid-call could still capture the token in the vendor-call function's stack; that residual is accepted given the window is now one function call, not a cache TTL (core-dump suppression at the runtime level is a `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md` mechanism, requirement recorded here, not owned here). The token field is excluded from any generic request/response dump used in error handling — the same log-hygiene discipline below applies to panic traces and debug dumps, not just application logs.
21	- **The outbound HTTP client to the vendor must construct a fresh, isolated `Authorization` header per request, never inherited from connection reuse.** Connection pooling/keep-alive is the normal default in any HTTP client library; a pooled client that reuses a request-context object or fails to reset the auth header per call can attach request A's vendor token to request B's outbound call — the same cross-tenant leak as a cache-key collision, with no cache required. This is a required implementation discipline, not an assumption left implicit.
22	- **Scoped narrowly if the vendor supports it** — request only the scope needed for `/identity`, not a broad token, so a leaked token's blast radius is bounded by the vendor's own authorization model as well as ours.
23	- **Returned once, to the authenticated internal caller only — never to end users, never persisted server-side (resolved by the cross-track consistency pass, F3).** Our own `/auth` returns the vendor `access_token` to whichever authenticated `connector:identity-lookup`-scoped caller invoked it (in this project's shape, `cmd/api-service` acting on an onboarding flow's behalf) — this is what the assignment's `/auth` contract actually requires ("retrieves an `access_token`"), and it is what `../01-product-industry-research-design/ux-notes.md`'s onboarding flow depends on to bridge its two user-facing steps. Because our own `/identity` request body is fixed by the assignment to `{"phone", "name"}` with no credential field, the vendor token travels as a request header on the subsequent `/identity` call (e.g. `X-Vendor-Access-Token`) — this doesn't violate the fixed JSON contract, which governs the body only, not headers. The connector itself still never caches or persists the token (S3's no-cache ruling stands unchanged): each `/identity` call's presented token is used once and discarded, exactly as if it had been freshly fetched, and it is the caller's responsibility — not the connector's — to hold the token for the short span between the two calls. If a caller presents no token, or an expired one, `/identity` fails closed with a generic error that does not distinguish "no token" from "bad token" from "vendor rejected it" (same user-enumeration discipline as §4's other failure-handling rules).
24	
25	### 2. How `/identity` calls are authenticated to the vendor
26	
27	- Standard bearer-token usage: the vendor `access_token` from `/auth` presented as an `Authorization: Bearer` header to the vendor's identity endpoint (or whatever the vendor's own contract specifies — this connector adapts to the vendor, not the reverse).
28	- **Token refresh/re-auth on expiry** handled by the connector transparently — a caller of our `/identity` endpoint should not need to know or manage vendor token lifecycle.
29	- **Vendor calls over TLS only**, certificate validation never disabled, including in local/dev environments (repeating the threat model's point because it's the most common place a "temporary" dev bypass ships to production unnoticed).
30	- **Our own credentials to call the vendor's `/auth`** (this connector's client id/secret with ABC/XYZ, if the vendor requires connector-level registration in addition to the end-user's own username/password) are runtime-injected secrets, never in source or config committed to the repo — requirement set here, mechanism owned by `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`.
31	
32	### 3. What this service should and shouldn't log or persist
33	
34	| Data | Log it? | Persist it? |
35	|---|---|---|
36	| End-user's vendor username | No (identifier, but low sensitivity relative to password — still avoid in plaintext logs; use it only for correlation via a hash if audit trails need it) | Only if a legitimate business reason exists; not by default |
37	| End-user's vendor password (`/auth` request body) | **Never, under any circumstance, including error paths and stack traces** | **Never** |
38	| Vendor `access_token` | **Never** | Only as short-TTL encrypted cache per above; never in plaintext, never in application logs |
39	| PII returned by `/identity` (name, phone, address fields) | Never the field values; log only the *fact* of a lookup (caller identity, timestamp, success/failure, which record was resolved by opaque id) | Retention/caching policy for this PII is `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`'s call — this track's requirement is that whatever is persisted follows the same encryption-at-rest and access-control bar as `user_profile` data, no weaker because it arrived via a connector rather than direct input |
40	| Vendor error responses | Log status/error-code only; do not log raw vendor response bodies by default, since a vendor error payload can itself echo back submitted PII or partial credentials | N/A |
41	
42	### 4. Failure handling as a security control, not just reliability
43	
44	- **Vendor auth failures must not leak which part of the credential was wrong** (username vs. password) in whatever this connector surfaces upward — same user-enumeration concern as the threat model's credential-stuffing row, now facing outward at whoever calls our `/auth` passthrough.
45	- **Retry/backoff on vendor calls** should be rate-limited on our side independent of the vendor's own limits, so a compromised or careless caller can't use our connector to hammer the vendor (which is both an availability risk to us and a way to get our connector's vendor credentials rate-limited or banned).
46	- **Circuit-breaking**: if the vendor is failing or behaving anomalously (e.g., returning tokens for identities that don't match the request), fail closed — do not fall back to a cached or stale identity result silently, since that risks serving stale PII as if freshly verified.
47	
48	### 5. Authentication and authorization of calls *into* this connector
49	
50	Found by Ingrid Solano's S8 independent review — §1–§4 above are entirely about protecting the *vendor* token and the *vendor* call; none of them state who is allowed to call *our* `/auth` and `/identity` in the first place. Fixed here rather than left as a silent assumption:
51	
52	- **Callers of `cmd/idp-connector`'s own `/auth` and `/identity` must be authenticated**, using the same OAuth2 client-credentials → JWT bearer mechanism as `api-auth-design.md`, but with a **distinct audience** (`aud: idp-connector-service`, separate from `loginid-api-service`) and its own scope (`connector:identity-lookup`) — this is a different threat model and a different, smaller caller set (internal services, not question 2's broader API clientele), so it gets its own audience rather than reusing the search API's. Only a service that legitimately mediates an end-user or business flow needing vendor identity data (in this project's shape, `cmd/api-service` acting on a caller's behalf) should hold this credential — it is not exposed to arbitrary external callers.
53	- **Why this matters concretely:** without it, an unauthenticated caller could hit our `/auth` passthrough with arbitrary vendor credentials all day and use this connector as exactly the credential-testing oracle §4's rate-limit control is meant to prevent — but "rate-limited per calling-API-client" has nothing to bind "per calling-API-client" to if no caller identity is required to reach the endpoint at all.
54	- **Audit logging** for calls into this connector follows the same discipline as §3's table: caller `sub`, scope, timestamp, outcome — never the vendor credentials or PII in the request/response bodies.
55	
56	### 6. What a vendor token authorizes at `/identity` — an assumption, stated explicitly
57	
58	Also found by S8 review. This design's token-handling discipline (§1) is entirely about *custody* of the vendor token — encryption, memory scope, never-log. It says nothing about what the token is allowed to *do*: specifically, whether a vendor `access_token` obtained from one person's `/auth` call authorizes an `/identity` query for a *different* person's `name`/`phone`, or only for the token-holder's own identity.
59	
60	This connector cannot enforce that boundary itself — the assignment's generic `/identity` contract (`{"phone": "...", "name": "..."}`, no scoping field) puts that decision entirely on the vendor's own API. **Stated assumption, not a resolved control:** this design assumes a real ABC/XYZ-shaped vendor binds `/identity` queries to the authenticated principal at the vendor side (i.e., the vendor's own API rejects a query for an identity other than the token-holder's). If that assumption doesn't hold for a given real vendor, this connector becomes a lookup oracle for anyone who can obtain one valid vendor token, and no control in §1–§5 touches that risk, because none of them govern token *authority* rather than token *custody*. This is the same class of question as the search API's object-level authorization (`api-auth-design.md`, S4) and belongs on `../01-product-industry-research-design/CLAUDE.md`'s list when evaluating which real vendor ABC/XYZ represents — this track cannot resolve it without knowing a concrete vendor's actual token semantics.
61	
62	## What this design does not resolve
63	
64	- Data retention duration for cached `/identity` results — `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`.
65	- Which secrets manager holds this connector's own vendor credentials — `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`.
66	- Vendor selection/evaluation (which real-world providers "ABC"/"XYZ" might represent) — that's product framing in `../01-product-industry-research-design/CLAUDE.md`, not a security question.
67	
68	## Sources
69	
70	- OWASP API Security Top 10 2023, API7 (Server Side Request Forgery) and API8 (Security Misconfiguration) — both relevant to a service that makes outbound calls to a third party on a user's behalf: https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0x11-t10/
71	- OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet (what not to log — credentials, tokens, PII): https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Logging_Cheat_Sheet.html
72	
73	## Attack-tree table — connector token lifecycle (S3 red/blue, 2026-09-12)
74	
75	Full positions, rebuttals, and ruling: `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`. Table shape per `planning-approach.md` Appendix A.
76	
77	| # | Leaf (attack path) | Precondition | Likelihood | Impact | Blue response: Control *or* Accepted risk | Owner | Residual | Source |
78	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
79	| 1 | Cache-key confusion → cross-tenant token/PII leak via request-derived cache key | Persistent cache (eliminated by default posture below) | M | H | **Eliminated by design:** no cache by default (Felix Adebayo, alternatives seat) — no persistent cache means no key to collide on. **Control, exception-path only:** if caching is ever re-opened, key must derive from immutable vendor-issued ids, never request-supplied `name`/`phone`. **Control (this run, Tomasz's rebuttal):** the connection-reuse variant of this leaf survives even with no cache — a pooled HTTP client that doesn't reset `Authorization` per call can attach one request's token to another. Closed with a required control: fresh, isolated auth header constructed per outbound request, never inherited from connection reuse. | 03 (client construction; cache-key derivation if exception ever opened) | **L** — storage variant eliminated; connection-reuse variant closed by an explicit, checkable implementation requirement | OWASP API8:2023; judgment (connection-reuse variant) |
80	| 2 | Fail-open on KMS unavailability during a caching window | Persistent cache with KMS-backed encryption at rest (eliminated by default posture) | M | H | **Eliminated by design:** no cache by default removes the write-time KMS dependency from the hot path. **Control, exception-path only:** if caching is ever re-opened, KMS-unreachable fails closed to "no cache, fetch live," never to an unencrypted write. Tomasz conceded this leaf outright — no hole found. | 03/04 (KMS mechanism is 04's) | **L** | NIST SP 800-57 |
81	| 3 | Vendor token replay — no proof-of-possession/session binding once decrypted for use | Token exfiltrated by any other means within its exposure window | M | H | **Accepted risk.** Bearer tokens under RFC 6750 are possession-equals-authorization by design; PoP/binding (DPoP, RFC 9449; mTLS-bound tokens, RFC 8705) needs vendor-side support this project cannot assume for a generic ABC/XYZ vendor the assignment never specifies. Acceptable *for this system* because fetch-per-call bounds the exposure window to one request's execution scope — no standing cached credential to steal. **Tightened per Tomasz's rebuttal:** the boundary is stated explicitly — fetch → use in the immediately following `/identity` call → zeroize; a retried `/identity` call re-fetches via `/auth` and never reuses a token from a prior attempt, so the accepted window cannot silently stretch to the length of the retry/backoff policy. | — (re-review trigger: a PoP-capable vendor surfacing in 01's research) | **M** — accepted, not eliminated; a real vendor-side constraint, not a design gap, but still "possession = authority" for the fetch's lifetime | RFC 6750 §5; RFC 9700 §2.5 |
82	| 4 | Process-memory/crash-dump exposure of the plaintext token before encryption | Attacker has read access to a crash artifact or swap on the host | L | M | **Control:** token field excluded from any generic request/response dump used in error handling (panic traces, debug dumps — same discipline as application logs); token variable scoped to the narrowest function possible, never passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function. **Residual accepted:** Go gives no guaranteed memory zeroing without unsafe/cgo, so a crash dump taken mid-call could still capture it; acceptable given the window narrowed from a cache TTL to one function call (Felix's alternatives turn). Core-dump suppression at the runtime level is a 04 mechanism, requirement recorded, not resolved here. | 03 (code discipline) / 04 (runtime config) | **L** — narrowed from a standing cache exposure to a single function-call window | OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet |
83	| 5 | Connector used as a credential-stuffing oracle against the vendor (rate-limit granularity unstated) | No per-identity/per-IP rate-limit granularity stated in the original design | M | M | **Control:** rate limit keyed per calling-API-client *and* per source IP, with a stricter threshold on vendor-auth *failure ratio* specifically (credential stuffing shows as a high failure ratio before high volume), paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted, not just per client — closes the granularity gap without adding a new requirement beyond what §4 already gestured at. | 03 (rate-limit logic) / 04 (shared rate-limit store or edge mechanism, if enforced at a gateway) | **L** | OWASP API4:2023; NIST SP 800-63B §5.2.2 |
84	
85	Rules of use followed: Tomasz ranked and handed over his top five before Helena answered (per `CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩); Felix's alternatives turn sat between red and blue and eliminated leaves 1's storage variant and 2's substrate by design change rather than control; Helena answered every leaf with a control or a justified accepted risk; Tomasz's rebuttal turn on the top three surfaced one unaddressed variant (leaf 1's connection-reuse case) and one boundary needing explicit statement (leaf 3), both resolved without reopening the ruling; Marcus assigned Residual with the justification in each row. No Residual **H** — none of the five leaves cleared that bar after blue's response and the rebuttal-turn tightening, so no follow-up Story is required; leaf 3's **M** is the one residual worth Warren's attention as a genuine accepted risk, not a gap.

1	# Hand-off 02 → 03: Auth scheme, validation point, and authorization requirements
2	
3	Status: **v3** — updated after the cross-track consistency pass (`../decisions/cross-track-consistency.md`) resolved six findings owned by this track that touched this hand-off (F3, F5, F6, F8, F10, F-pag, F52); v2's S8-driven fixes are unchanged and folded in below as before. Receiver: Renata Cole, `../03-engineering-delivery/`. Written so 03 should be able to scaffold and build the middleware without re-deriving the reasoning in `api-auth-design.md`, `connector-security.md`, or the S3/S4/S8 decision records. Full reasoning, if wanted: `api-auth-design.md`, `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`, `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`.
4	
5	## Token scheme
6	
7	- **Grant type:** OAuth2 client-credentials. Machine-to-machine only; no end-user login flow for question 2's API.
8	- **Token format:** JWT, signed, asymmetric (RS256 or ES256 — pick one and use it consistently; RS256 is the safer default if your Go JWT library's ES256 support is less mature).
9	- **Claim set (minimum):** `sub` (calling client identity), `scope` (space-delimited, values below), `exp`, `iat`, `aud` (this API, specifically — reject tokens minted for another audience).
10	- **Literal values, filling the "pending 02's final scheme" placeholder in your Service boundaries config surface:** `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER = "https://auth.loginid-takehome.internal"`, `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE = "loginid-api-service"`. Both are configuration, not secrets — safe in a `ConfigMap` or committed default, per `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3.
11	- **TTL:** 5–15 minutes. No refresh tokens — the client re-authenticates with its own client secret on expiry.
12	- **No denylist.** Revocation is handled by TTL shortness, not a stateful lookup. Don't build one; it's not missing, it's a deliberate trade-off already reasoned through in `api-auth-design.md`.
13	- **Verifier must pin the expected algorithm** (whichever of RS256/ES256 you choose) and reject a token whose header claims any other algorithm, including `alg: none` and a downgrade to a symmetric algorithm using the public key as an HMAC secret (RFC 8725 §3.1). Check your JWT library's default behavior — several popular libraries have shipped this vulnerability by not doing this by default.
14	- **Token-endpoint brute-force protection**, separate from the resource-API rate limits below: failed-grant rate limiting and progressive backoff per `client_id` and per source IP on the token-issuing endpoint itself, plus alerting on a sustained run of failed grants against one `client_id`. This protects the endpoint where a caller presents `client_id`/`client_secret`, which none of the resource-API limits below cover.
15	- **Bearer-token replay is an accepted risk, not a gap to close in implementation.** This token has the same possession-equals-authority property as the connector's vendor token (`connector-security.md`); no PoP/binding is expected or required here. Noted so it isn't mistaken for something to "fix" during implementation.
16	
17	## Where it's validated
18	
19	- **Middleware at the API edge**, in `internal/api/` per your published service boundary — every request to `cmd/api-service` passes through token validation before reaching a handler. `cmd/idp-connector` does not validate *this* token — but per `connector-security.md` §5 (added by S8 review), `idp-connector`'s own `/auth` and `/identity` handlers need the same JWT middleware pattern, with a **distinct audience** (`aud: idp-connector-service`, not `loginid-api-service`) and its own scope (`connector:identity-lookup`), restricted to internal callers (in this project's shape, `api-service` acting on a caller's behalf) — not the same token, not exposed to arbitrary external callers. Two audiences, one mechanism.
20	- **Key retrieval:** middleware verifies with the **public** key only (JWKS or a fetched public key — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3). The private signing key never touches `api-service`; it lives with the token-issuing function, which is a mode of `api-service` (not a separate binary — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` Assumption 2), scoped by RBAC so the verification code path and the issuing code path don't share Secret access even though they're the same binary.
21	- **Key rotation:** expect a `kid` claim; publish old and new public keys together during rotation overlap. Token TTL (5–15 min) bounds how long that overlap needs to last.
22	
23	## Scope vocabulary (final, from S4 — `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`)
24	
25	| Scope | Authorizes | Does not authorize | Object-level check |
26	|---|---|---|---|
27	| `profile:read:own` | Retrieve a single record by id the caller already holds a legitimate reference to (e.g., a partner resolving a referred user) | Search/query by name or phone fragment; retrieval of a record outside the caller's referral relationship | `target.id` checked against the `caller_referral` mapping (see "Where `caller_referral` and the caller-role table live," below); out-of-set → reject, logged as a policy denial, distinct from an auth failure |
28	| `profile:read:any` | Retrieve a single record by id, no referral constraint | Search/query by fragment | Requires a `reason_code` from a closed enum (below) on every request, logged with the audit record |
29	| `profile:search` | Query by name/phone fragment across the table | — (this is the broadest scope; there is no broader one) | Result cap enforced; a query that would exceed it unconstrained (e.g., bare wildcard) is rejected outright, not truncated |
30	
31	`reason_code` enum for `profile:read:any` (closed, versioned — adding a value is a documented change, not a runtime config toggle): `support_ticket`, `fraud_review`, `kyc_reverification`, `legal_hold`.
32	
33	**Object-level policy hook implementation shape:** a function `authorize(sub, scope, target) -> allow|deny`, called after scope validation, before the handler executes the query/retrieve. This is a second check, not a replacement for the scope check — a valid `profile:read:own` token can still fail `authorize()` if the target isn't in the caller's referral set. **Confirmed placement (per your reply): an explicit call inside each handler right after parsing the request and before the DAO call, not a second middleware** — correct, since `authorize()` needs the parsed target id, which a pre-routing middleware doesn't have. **Enforcement site for the `profile:search` result cap: the same in-handler call site, checked before the query is built** — a query that would exceed the cap unconstrained is rejected there, not truncated after the DAO returns rows.
34	
35	**Where `caller_referral` and the caller-role table live (resolves F8 — this data was previously asserted as "owned by `../05-data-ops/`" without 05 ever having received that assignment; 05's schema has no such table and shouldn't grow one for this).** Both live in the token-issuing authorization server's own datastore (the same one that holds the hashed `client_secret`s per `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #4), not in `05-data-ops`'s `user_profile`/`user_credential` schema. This is authorization-provisioning data — who a client credential is allowed to act on behalf of — not subject data, and keeping it out of 05's schema also keeps it out of that schema's PII-governance/retention rules, which don't apply to it. 05 owns nothing here; this hand-off's earlier wording was wrong to say otherwise.
36	
37	**Response shape and masking (resolves F6 — 01's product requirement had no mechanism to bind to).** `profile:search` responses carry masked PII fields by default (e.g., a phone shown as `***-***-1234`, an address reduced to `locality`/`region` only) — unmasked, full-field detail is obtained only by a follow-up call under `profile:read:own` or `profile:read:any` against the specific record id returned by the search, which is its own separately-audited call per the audit-log section below. This uses scopes that already exist rather than inventing a fourth "expand" concept: masking is a `profile:search`-response property, not a per-call flag, and "tighter than search" is satisfied structurally because `read:own`/`read:any` are already narrower-provisioned scopes than `search`.
38	
39	**Reject an empty search body before the DAO (resolves F10, first half).** A `profile:search` request with no `name` or `phone` filter at all is rejected by the handler with a 400 before it ever reaches the DAO — the DAO's own "an all-nil query is legal and returns every profile" behavior (`../05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md`) is a DAO-layer property this API must not expose directly. This is a shape-based rejection, distinct from and in addition to the count-based result-cap rejection above.
40	
41	## Pagination, rate limits, decomposition resistance (requirements — numbers are fixed, mechanism is yours)
42	
43	- **Opaque cursor at the API boundary** (resolves F-pag — the earlier "cursor-based pagination only, no offset-based paging" was wrong to imply offset itself is the enumeration risk: a keyset cursor permits the same page-by-page walk, just statefully; the actual control is the cumulative counter below, and 05's DAO contract is `Offset int`, not keyset). The API-facing cursor may encode the DAO's offset directly — callers cannot construct or walk one by hand, which is the property the boundary exists to give you, and the DAO's offset is never caller-visible. **Enforcement site: the cursor-encoding/decoding step in the handler layer**, opaque to the caller regardless of what it encodes underneath. Default page size 20, hard ceiling 50 (05's DAO ceiling is 10,000 — this API's ceiling is the tighter, caller-facing one; don't expose the DAO's).
44	- **Per-client-credential rate limits:** 60 req/min for `read:own`/`read:any`; 10 req/min for `profile:search`. **Enforcement site: rate-limiting middleware in `internal/api/`, keyed per client credential (`sub` claim).**
45	- **Cumulative distinct-record-touch counter per caller per rolling 24h window**, tracked independently of per-minute rate — this is the control that actually resists decomposition-style enumeration (not the pagination scheme, per the F-pag correction above). **Applies to `profile:read:any` as well as `profile:search`, unconditionally on whether a `reason_code` is present** — a valid, logged `reason_code` satisfies the per-call `authorize()` policy check but does not exempt the caller from this cumulative cap (S8 review fix: the original wording made this counter a no-op for `read:any`, since that scope requires a `reason_code` on every call by design). **Enforcement site: a counting store (Redis, in-process with periodic flush, whatever fits your stack) incremented once per distinct record id returned, checked before the response is sent** — the mechanism is your call; the requirement is that it exists, applies to both scopes, and alerts on threshold, not just hard-blocks.
46	- Sustained near-limit usage on any of the above should alert, not just silently throttle. **Enforcement site: the same rate-limiting middleware and counting store above, not a separate mechanism.**
47	
48	## Audit-log field list (every search/retrieve call)
49	
50	Log these fields, and only these — see `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list for what must never appear here:
51	
52	- `sub` (caller identity)
53	- `scope` used
54	- record id(s) touched (opaque ids, not the PII values)
55	- `reason_code`, when the scope is `profile:read:any`
56	- policy decision (`allow` / `deny`, and if `deny`, whether it was a scope failure or an `authorize()` policy denial — these are distinct signals for detection)
57	- timestamp
58	- request outcome (success/failure/rate-limited)
59	
60	Never in this log: PII field values, full JWTs, full request/response bodies. Per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s new requirement, this log stream itself needs read access distinct from and narrower than whatever can read Secrets — that's 04's mechanism, but don't let the log ship without asking whether that separation exists.
61	
62	## What's not yours to decide (already decided upstream, don't re-derive)
63	
64	- **Grant type, token format, and claim set** — decided in `api-auth-design.md`; not open for re-litigation during implementation.
65	- **Scope vocabulary and provisioning bars** — decided in S4 (`decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`); if a real implementation constraint makes one of these unworkable, flag it back to me rather than adjusting it unilaterally.
66	- **The `DB_DSN_FILE` convention** — per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s S7 revision, this track is asking you to add a file-based alternative to `DB_DSN` (env var becomes a transitional fallback, not a permanent equal option), because a DSN's embedded password is exposed by `kubectl describe`/crash dumps/child-process environment the same as any other secret in that inventory. This is a request into your config surface, not a unilateral change on my part — you decide the exact mechanism (a `_FILE` suffix convention or otherwise), I'm asserting the requirement.
67	- **Password/client-secret hashing** — Argon2id, decided in `threat-model.md` Asset 1; not this hand-off's concern beyond noting it exists.
68	- **PII retention/caching duration for anything the API returns** — `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`'s call, not this track's or yours.
69	- **Whether `read:any` ever gets issued to a real caller** — per S4, the scope stays defined but unissued unless a caller clears the provisioning bar (caller-role table entry, named approver). Don't wire a default grant "just in case."
70	
71	## Open question back to you
72	
73	Nothing blocking. One thing worth a quick confirm when convenient: does your `internal/api/` middleware layer have a natural place to run the object-level `authorize()` check as a distinct step from scope validation (e.g., a second middleware or an explicit call inside each handler before the DAO call), or would you rather this track propose a specific Go interface shape for it? Either is fine — say which you'd find more useful and I'll adjust.
74

1	# Hand-off 02 → 04: Secrets inventory and never-log list
2	
3	Status: **v2** — v1's S7 objection turn and 04's Newcomer cold read are both resolved and folded in below; this revision additionally resolves four findings from the cross-track consistency pass (F12 this version label, F13 the signing-key custody contradiction, F15 a missing credential row, F50 two never-log gaps). Receiver: Theo Bergman, `../04-infra-devops/`. Written to the project's hand-off standard: 04 should be able to act on this without re-deriving the reasoning in `threat-model.md`, `api-auth-design.md`, or `connector-security.md`.
4	
5	## Assumptions this hand-off makes
6	
7	1. Deployment target is the **design-only Kubernetes lab cluster** 04 names (Warren's decision via Theo, `../LOG.md`). Delivery mechanism is therefore Kubernetes-native `Secret` objects unless 04 chooses an external store; every requirement below is written so it still holds if the mechanism changes.
8	2. Service topology is 03's published boundary (`../PLANNING.md`, Service boundaries): two binaries, `cmd/api-service` (Q1 DAO + Q2 REST API) and `cmd/idp-connector` (Q3), env-var configuration only. Variable names below are 03's; two are marked as pending this track's final scheme. **The token-issuing authorization-server function is a mode of `api-service`, not a third binary** — this take-home's scope doesn't justify a standalone authz server, and 03 has published no third `cmd/` entrypoint. **Corrected by the cross-track consistency pass (F13):** "a mode of the same binary" is not "the same Kubernetes Deployment," and the earlier text here was wrong to imply RBAC alone separates them — a Kubernetes Role restricting `get` on a Secret has no effect on a volume already mounted into a container, so if `api-service`'s one Deployment mounted the signing key, every replica (verification path included) would have it on disk regardless of RBAC. **The fix: the issuer runs as a second Deployment of the same `api-service` image**, selected by an environment variable 03 adds to the config surface (e.g. `APP_MODE=issuer` vs. the default, consistent with the surface's env-var-only, no-flags rule), with **its own ServiceAccount that alone mounts the signing-key Secret** (row #2) — the `api-service` Deployment serving verification/search mounts nothing from row #2. This is a request into 03's Deployment/mode surface, not a unilateral change on my part; flag to me if the mode-selection mechanism needs to differ.
9	3. An **external operator agent** — a human or automated actor outside this project, holding cluster-admin or namespace-admin rights on 04's design-only lab cluster, who applies Kubernetes manifests to it — is a privileged actor outside this service: anyone who can apply a manifest can read any Secret it mounts. Consequence for 04: manifests reference Secrets by name and never carry values; values are set out-of-band. This is a named trust boundary in `threat-model.md`, not a silent assumption; nothing further about who that operator is needs to be re-derived from 04's own files to act on this hand-off.
10	4. Nothing is deployed and nobody contacts the cluster or its operator during the take-home. This document describes what *would* be delivered, not what has been.
11	
12	## Secrets inventory
13	
14	| # | Secret | Used by | Injection requirement (02 sets it) | Rotation expectation | Kubernetes-native note (04's mechanism) | Never-log |
15	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
16	| 1 | Database credentials, carried inside `DB_DSN` | `api-service` | Runtime-injected; never in source, image, or committed config. **Required, not preferred: file mount via a `DB_DSN_FILE` convention** (03 to add alongside `DB_DSN`), because a DSN's embedded password is exposed by `kubectl describe`, crash dumps, and child-process environment exactly as much as any other secret in this table — there is no citation that makes `DB_DSN` a lesser case, so this track is not treating it as one | On credential rotation; service restart or reload acceptable | `Secret` mounted read-only as a file, read via `DB_DSN_FILE`; env-var `DB_DSN` is a fallback only until 03 ships the file-based convention, not a permanent equal option | Yes — the DSN contains the password; log the driver name and host at most |
17	| 2 | JWT signing **private** key (RS256/ES256), per `api-auth-design.md` | The issuer Deployment (`api-service` image, `APP_MODE=issuer`) only — **not** the `api-service` Deployment that verifies with the public key; these are two Deployments of one image, not one Deployment doing both (F13 fix) | Runtime-injected as a **file**, never an env var; asymmetric so the verifying Deployment never holds it | Key rotation with a `kid` claim; publish old and new public keys together during overlap; TTL of tokens (5–15 min) bounds the overlap window | `Secret` mounted read-only as a file, **only into the issuer Deployment's pod spec** (not the verifying Deployment's); RBAC restricts `get`/`list` to the issuer's own ServiceAccount | Yes |
18	| 3 | JWT **public** key / JWKS | `api-service` middleware | Not a secret; may ship in config or be fetched from the issuer | Follows #2 | Plain `ConfigMap` or fetched URL; listed here so 04 does not over-protect it | No (but never log full tokens — see below) |
19	| 4 | API caller `client_secret`s (OAuth2 client-credentials) | Authorization server (verifies); callers hold their own copy | **Stored hashed** (Argon2id, same floor as `threat-model.md` Asset 1), never plaintext at rest; issued once at provisioning and not recoverable | Per-client rotation on demand; revoke by deleting the hash | Lives in the issuer's datastore, not a Kubernetes `Secret`; 04 delivers only that datastore's credentials (#1-shaped) | Yes |
20	| 5 | Connector's own vendor registration: `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_ID`, `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_SECRET` (and the XYZ pair if a second vendor is configured) | `idp-connector` | Runtime-injected; never in source or committed config; `client_id` is low-sensitivity but travels with the secret and gets the same handling | Per vendor's policy; expect manual rotation | One `Secret` per vendor so rotating ABC never touches XYZ; file mount preferred | Yes for the secret; keep the id out of logs too |
21	| 6 | Vendor `access_token` (obtained via `/auth`, used for `/identity`) | `idp-connector`, in memory | **Not a deployed secret** — transient. If cached at all: TTL ≤ vendor expiry, encrypted at rest with #7, keyed per vendor account (`connector-security.md` §1) | Expires on its own; never persisted across restarts | No `Secret` object; if 04 provisions a cache, it must be encrypted using #7 and reachable only from `idp-connector` | Yes — never, including error paths |
22	| 7 | Data-encryption key (KEK) for envelope encryption of any TOTP shared secrets and any cached vendor tokens | `api-service` (TOTP) — **not a committed feature of this submission**; `user_credential.method` is modeled as an extensible lookup table (`../05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md`) that supports TOTP as a *possible* value, but no TOTP implementation is planned unless 03 builds one. `idp-connector` (token cache) — **also not committed**: S3's red/blue on the connector token lifecycle (in progress) is deciding whether the connector caches vendor tokens at all; current direction (Felix Adebayo's alternatives turn) is "no cache by default, fetched fresh per `/identity` call," which would remove this use case too. This row is provisioned only if either feature materializes — it is a contingency the inventory names in advance, not a live requirement for 04 to build now | Runtime-injected as a file; the KEK never leaves the process that decrypts | Rotation by re-wrapping DEKs; never requires re-hashing passwords (those are hashed, not encrypted) | `Secret` (file mount) or an external KMS if 04 prefers — the requirement is only that a KEK exists and is not in source | Yes |
23	| 7a | Credential to reach the external KMS, **only if 04 exercises the KMS alternative in row #7** (a role, API key, or client cert — whichever the chosen KMS uses) | Whichever binary holds the KEK reference (#7) | Same discipline as every other row here: runtime-injected, never in source, never provisioned ad hoc outside this table | Per the KMS provider's own rotation mechanism | Cloud-KMS access is typically workload-identity-based (no static credential to store) rather than a `Secret` object; if the chosen KMS instead requires a static credential, it gets its own `Secret` under the same RBAC discipline as row #2 | Yes |
24	| 8 | TLS serving private keys for `api-service` and `idp-connector` | Ingress / each binary, per 04's termination choice | Runtime-injected; TLS 1.2+ only; certificate validation never disabled in any environment (`connector-security.md` §2) | Per certificate lifetime; automated issuance preferred | `kubernetes.io/tls` `Secret`; issuance mechanism is 04's call | Yes |
25	| 9 | End-user's vendor password (`POST /auth` request body) | `idp-connector`, transient | Never persisted, never cached, held only for the vendor call | n/a | Nothing for 04 to deliver; listed so nobody creates a place for it | **Yes — under no circumstance, including stack traces** |
26	| 10 | `api-service`'s own client credential (`client_id`/`client_secret`) toward the issuer, scoped `connector:identity-lookup`/`aud: idp-connector-service` — required for `api-service` to call `idp-connector`'s own authenticated `/auth`/`/identity` (`connector-security.md` §5) | `api-service` (the Deployment serving question 2's API, acting as a caller of the connector on a user's behalf) | Runtime-injected; never in source or committed config — same discipline as row 5's vendor registration, added by the cross-track consistency pass (F15), which found this credential asserted as a requirement in `connector-security.md`/`handoff-03-auth.md` with no inventory row | Same per-client rotation as row 4 (revoke by deleting the issuer-side hash) | File mount, one `Secret`, `api-service`'s own ServiceAccount | Yes |
27	
28	Names marked pending in 03's boundary: `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER` / `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` are configuration, not secrets; this track will confirm their final values in `handoff-03-auth.md`. They are listed here only so 04 does not classify them as secrets.
29	
30	## Kubernetes-specific requirements (04 implements; 02 sets)
31	
32	- **Secrets at rest in etcd are base64, not encrypted, by default.** Either enable encryption at rest for the `Secret` resource type, or source values from an external store that never writes plaintext to etcd. One or the other; the design must say which.
33	- **RBAC:** no service account may `get`/`list` Secrets it does not mount. The operator agent's apply rights are a documented trust boundary, not a hole to close in this take-home — but the design should note that its scope could be narrowed to a namespace.
34	- **Manifests carry references, never values.** Any manifest in the repository contains Secret *names*; values are provided out-of-band. This is what keeps assumption 3 honest.
35	- **Prefer file mounts over env vars** for #2, #5, #7, #8. #1 is env-var only because 03's config surface is env-var only; if 03 accepts a `_FILE` suffix convention, move it.
36	- **No secret in an image layer, build arg, or CI log.** CI redaction is 04's mechanism; the requirement is that a build log can be published without exposing anything in this table.
37	- **Audit/access logs (the output of the never-log list below) are readable only by an incident-review role, distinct from and narrower than general operator access.** `sub`, scope, record id, and timestamp are enough on their own to reconstruct who looked up whom — that log stream needs its own read restriction, separate from whichever service account can `get`/`list` the Secrets in this table. Mechanism (log-pipeline RBAC, a separate log sink with its own access control, etc.) is 04's call; the requirement is that "can read Secrets" and "can read the audit trail" are not the same grant.
38	
39	## Never-log list (applies to every component, every environment, every log level, including error paths and stack traces)
40	
41	1. End-user vendor password (#9).
42	2. Vendor `access_token` (#6) — and any `Authorization` header value, inbound or outbound, in full.
43	3. PII field values from `user_profile` or from `/identity` responses: name, phone, `street_address`, `locality`, `region`, `postal_code`, `country`. Log the *fact* of access — caller `sub`, scope used, opaque record id, timestamp, status — never the values.
44	4. Raw vendor error response bodies (they can echo submitted PII or partial credentials). Log status code and vendor error code only.
45	5. JWT signing private key (#2) and any JWT in full — log `sub`, `aud`, `exp`, and `kid` if needed, never the token.
46	6. API caller `client_secret`s (#4), in plaintext or hashed.
47	7. `DB_DSN` (#1) or any connection string containing credentials.
48	8. Full request bodies of `POST /auth` and `POST /identity` — both contain items above by construction.
49	9. Search query parameters containing a name or phone fragment (`api-auth-design.md`, hardening) — they are PII; redact or hash before any access log, including proxy and ingress logs 04 controls.
50	10. **Database driver error messages and `DETAIL`/`Key (...)=(...)` text** (added by the cross-track consistency pass, F50 — the most probable PII-to-log path in the system and previously named by no item on this list). A Postgres CHECK or unique-constraint violation on a `user_profile`/`user_credential` write echoes the failing row's column values, or the offending key value, in its error `DETAIL`. Log SQLSTATE/result code and constraint name only — the sanitization happens in 03's error-translation layer (`error-semantics.md`) before the error is wrapped for logging, not after.
51	11. **Username** (added by the same pass, F50) — typically an email address, and previously ungoverned by this list even though it can appear in `ErrDuplicateUsername` driver detail (item 10) or in a log line correlating a failed login. Treat it with the same discipline as a PII field value (item 3): log a hash or an opaque identifier for correlation, never the raw value.
52	
53	## What 04 does not decide (already decided upstream)
54	
55	Token TTLs and claim set (`api-auth-design.md`); scope vocabulary (`handoff-03-auth.md`, pending); password and client-secret hashing (`threat-model.md` Asset 1); whether the connector caches vendor tokens (`connector-security.md` §1 — it may, under the conditions in #6); PII retention windows (`../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`).
56	
57	## S7 objection turn — Ingrid Solano, resolved
58	
59	All four objections accepted; no decline.
60	
61	1. **Authorization-server ownership gap.** Accepted. Assumption 2 now states the authz-issuing function is a mode of `api-service`, scoped by a Role narrower than the token-verification code path in the same binary — not a phantom third deployable.
62	2. **Missing KMS-access-credential row.** Accepted. Added row #7a with the same columns as every other row, rather than leaving it as a footnote inside #7.
63	3. **Env-var exception for `DB_DSN`.** Accepted — the objection is correct that "prefer, if 03 allows" was this track declining a call it owns. Upgraded to a requirement: a `DB_DSN_FILE` convention, with the env var as a transitional fallback only. Carried into `handoff-03-auth.md` as an ask to 03.
64	4. **No RBAC on audit-log read access.** Accepted. Added a requirement under Kubernetes-specific requirements that Secret-read and audit-log-read are distinct grants.

1	# UX Notes — Two Surfaces on Top of This Backend
2	
3	Prepared for the `01-product-industry-research-design` track. See `../CLAUDE.md` for the assignment and `./CLAUDE.md` for this track's scope: this is light-touch, backend-supporting narrative, not a design deliverable in its own right — no wireframes, no design canvas. Text, one page, describing screens and the one design rule each surface enforces.
4	
5	## Surface 1: the admin profile-search console (Persona 2)
6	
7	**Who uses it:** the support/ops analyst from `personas-use-cases.md`, resolving a ticket or investigating a flagged transaction.
8	
9	**Screens:**
10	
11	- **Search screen.** A single form: name (with a partial-match toggle — phone is always exact match, no toggle needed, per the accepted product limitation in `personas-use-cases.md`), phone. No blank-query submit — the form requires at least one field filled before the search button is enabled, matching the "no unconstrained query" product requirement.
12	- **Results list.** Each row shows masked/partial PII by default (e.g., a partially redacted phone number, a name, no address) and an "expand" affordance per row, not a bulk-select checkbox anywhere on the screen.
13	- **Expanded record view.** Full PII for one record, reached only through the per-row expand action. This is a different screen state, not a hover-to-reveal or an inline toggle on the results list — the extra navigation step is deliberate and consistent with the backend treating expanded access as a distinct, auditable event.
14	
15	**The one design rule this surface enforces:** masked-by-default, expand-is-a-distinct-action. Every other screen decision — no bulk-select, expand requiring its own navigation step, no "export" button anywhere — is downstream of that one rule, not a separate design choice each time.
16	
17	## Surface 2: the onboarding pre-fill flow (Persona 3)
18	
19	**Who uses it:** a new user signing up for a service that offers "sign in with a partner provider" instead of a blank registration form, per Persona 3 in `personas-use-cases.md`.
20	
21	**Screens:**
22	
23	- **Provider choice.** A short list of partner providers (ABC, XYZ, ...), each a single button — the user picks one, consistent with the connector being provider-agnostic on the backend.
24	- **Partner credential entry.** Username/password fields, framed as "sign in to `<provider>`," not the service's own login form — visually distinct, to signal whose credential this is.
25	- **Pre-filled review screen.** Name, phone, and address arrive already filled in from the `/identity` response; the user reviews and confirms rather than retyping. Fields are editable, not read-only — the provider is a starting point, not an irrevocable source of truth for this signup.
26	
27	**The one design rule this surface enforces:** the partner's password is never visible to, or stored by, this service past the token exchange — the review screen never displays a password field, partner or otherwise, and nothing on this screen persists the credential entered on the previous one. That is the UI-level expression of the connector's `/auth` → `/identity` two-step: a short-lived token stands in for the password everywhere past the first screen. That token is held only by this onboarding flow, only for the span between the two calls, and is never persisted past this signup step — the connector that issues it holds it no longer than that either (`../02-ai-security-architecture/connector-security.md` §1).
28

input

  "file_path": "/Users/warrenthompson/Source/LoginID/01-product-industry-research-design/api-connector-design-rationale.md"
